ENTRY: 2.1.3 PRIMARY AUDITORY CORTEX (A1, HESCHL'S GYRUS, BRODMANN AREAS 41 AND 42)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: A1 differentiates on the superior temporal plane from the dorsal telencephalon, and its thalamic input is functional before birth: the cochlea works from roughly the 24th to 26th gestational week, and third-trimester fetuses respond to sound, which makes hearing the sense with the longest prenatal cortical experience. Heschl's gyrus is among the early-myelinating primary areas, maturing across infancy while the belt and parabelt around it mature later.

Its sensitive period is best documented through deprivation: congenitally deaf children given cochlear implants show near-normal cortical auditory responses if implanted within about the first 3.5 years, and progressively poorer outcomes later, the clearest dated sensitive period in human audition. Tonotopic organization is present at birth and refined by experience.

A1 matures before the auditory association cortex and before Wernicke's territory, in keeping with the primary-first gradient.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No common disorder has A1 as its primary seat. Bilateral destruction, usually by sequential strokes, produces cortical deafness, in which the region is a CASUALTY.

In tinnitus, reorganization of the tonotopic map after peripheral hearing loss is widely held to be the CAUSE of the phantom sound, though the model is debated and subcortical contributions are real. In schizophrenia, activation of A1 and the adjacent belt during auditory verbal hallucinations is a MARKER; the initiating fault lies in wider circuitry.

Age-related hearing loss is overwhelmingly peripheral; A1 is a downstream CASUALTY of deprivation, showing thinning with deafferentation. Its selective vulnerability profile is low.

ENTRY: 2.1.4 PRIMARY SOMATOSENSORY CORTEX (S1, POSTCENTRAL GYRUS, BRODMANN AREAS 3, 1, AND 2)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: S1 forms on the postcentral bank of the central sulcus from the dorsal telencephalon, and with M1 belongs to the earliest of Flechsig's myelinating areas; the thalamocortical somatosensory radiation is myelinating around term and matures over the first year. Somatosensory evoked responses are recordable in preterm infants, making touch cortically functional earlier than vision.

The homuncular map is assembled prenatally and refined by use throughout life; the sharpest experimental evidence for a critical period comes from the rodent barrel cortex, where whisker removal in the first postnatal days permanently deletes the corresponding barrels (rodent data). In humans the map remains plastic into adulthood, expanding with skilled use and contracting with disuse, so the sensitive period is broad rather than sharp.

S1 matures ahead of S2 and the posterior parietal cortex it feeds.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No degenerative disease has S1 as its primary seat. In middle cerebral artery stroke it is a CASUALTY, producing contralateral loss of discriminative touch and astereognosis.

In phantom limb phenomena, reorganization of the deafferented map is a MARKER of the amputation and, on the dominant account, a contributing CAUSE of phantom pain, though the causal reading has been challenged.

In focal task-specific dystonia, such as writer's cramp and musician's dystonia, degraded and overlapping S1 finger representations are a well-replicated finding, and the region is plausibly a CAUSE jointly with the basal ganglia. Map changes in complex regional pain syndrome are MARKERS. Vulnerability to late-life degeneration is low.

ENTRY: 2.1.5 PRIMARY MOTOR CORTEX (M1, PRECENTRAL GYRUS, BRODMANN AREA 4)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: M1 differentiates on the precentral gyrus from the dorsal telencephalon; its Betz cells are among the earliest cortical neurons to be born and the largest to mature. It is a Flechsig primordial area, yet its output cable matures slowly: corticospinal tract myelination begins before birth, supports independent walking around the end of the first year, and continues to refine through childhood, with the withdrawal of transient ipsilateral projections sharpening lateralized control across the first two years.

The motor map remains plastic lifelong under practice, dopamine-gated; there is no sharp critical period, but early injury is compensated far better than adult injury, the Kennard gradient. M1 matures with S1 and ahead of the premotor and prefrontal cortex that instruct it.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: M1 is the cortical primary seat of amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS): the Betz cells and their corticospinal axons degenerate, and on the corticofugal hypothesis the disease propagates from cortex down to the spinal motor neurons, making M1 a candidate CAUSE; at minimum it is an early and selective CASUALTY. The giant, high-demand Betz cell is the textbook example of selective neuronal vulnerability.

In stroke M1 is a CASUALTY, producing contralateral spastic paresis. In primary lateral sclerosis it is again the seat of upper motor neuron degeneration.

In Parkinson disease M1 output is disordered but the region itself is a CASUALTY of basal ganglia failure, not a cause. Excess beta-band drive from M1 is a MARKER of the parkinsonian state.

ENTRY: 2.1.6 PREMOTOR CORTEX (PMC) AND SUPPLEMENTARY MOTOR AREA (SMA), BRODMANN AREA 6

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: Area 6 arises immediately rostral to M1 from the same dorsal telencephalic sheet and matures after it, in Flechsig's intermediate wave: myelination and pruning here run through childhood and adolescence, later than area 4 but earlier than prefrontal cortex.

Its contribution to the corticospinal tract myelinates on the same slow schedule as M1's, and the capacity for internally generated, sequenced action that SMA supports develops across childhood with it. No discrete human critical period is established for area 6, and none should be claimed.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No degenerative disease has area 6 as its unique primary seat, but it is an early CASUALTY in corticobasal degeneration, where asymmetric premotor and peri-rolandic tau pathology produces apraxia and the alien limb phenomenon.

SMA-territory stroke or surgical resection produces the SMA syndrome, transient mutism and akinesia with recovery; the region is a CASUALTY. Seizures arising here produce hypermotor and fencing-posture attacks, and in that epilepsy the region is the CAUSE.

In Parkinson disease SMA hypoactivation during self-initiated movement is one of the most replicated imaging findings, a MARKER and proximate CASUALTY of the failed basal ganglia input. In stuttering and in Tourette syndrome, SMA and its loop show hyperactivity, a MARKER with a debated causal share.

ENTRY: 2.1.7 DORSOLATERAL PREFRONTAL CORTEX (DLPFC, BRODMANN AREAS 9 AND 46)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The DLPFC arises from the rostral dorsal telencephalon and sits at the far end of every maturational gradient: it is a Flechsig terminal zone, among the last cortex to myelinate, and its synaptic density peaks in early childhood and is then pruned through adolescence, with grey matter thinning and white matter growth continuing into the third decade and network reorganization measurable into the thirties. The claim that it finishes at 25 is the artefact named in this project's brief; the data show a long tail, not a deadline.

Its layer III association circuits, the substrate of working memory, are the slowest-maturing element. Adolescence functions as an extended sensitive period for the executive functions it hosts. It matures after the parietal and temporal regions that feed it.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The DLPFC is a primary seat of schizophrenia: reduced dendritic spine density on its layer III pyramidal neurons, altered parvalbumin interneurons, and working-memory hypofrontality make it a leading candidate CAUSE, with onset timed to its own adolescent pruning window. Its late-maturing, sparsely myelinated circuitry is the vulnerability profile.

In major depression, left DLPFC hypoactivity is a reliable MARKER and the region is the standard target of repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation; whether it is also a CAUSE is unresolved.

In attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), delayed cortical maturation with prefrontal peak thickness reached about three years late is a replicated MARKER. In frontotemporal dementia and normal ageing the DLPFC is an early CASUALTY, and executive function is the first cognitive domain to decline.

ENTRY: 2.1.8 VENTROMEDIAL PREFRONTAL CORTEX (VMPFC) AND ORBITOFRONTAL CORTEX (OFC), BRODMANN AREAS 10, 11, 12, 25, 32

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The VMPFC and OFC arise from the rostroventral dorsal telencephalon, with contributions at the margin from the ventral pallium; like the DLPFC they are Flechsig terminal zones, myelinating and pruning into the third decade. Value learning, reversal learning, and the regulation of the amygdala mature across adolescence on this anatomy.

Early life appears to be a sensitive period for this region's affective wiring: early adversity and institutional deprivation leave measurable structural change in OFC and its amygdala connections, though the window's boundaries are not sharply dated and should not be given false precision. It matures broadly in step with the DLPFC and after the limbic structures it must govern.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The OFC is mechanically the most vulnerable cortex in the skull: it rests on the orbital roof and is the classic CASUALTY of closed head injury, producing the Phineas Gage pattern of disinhibition with preserved intellect.

In behavioural-variant frontotemporal dementia, OFC, VMPFC, and anterior cingulate are the primary seat of degeneration; the region is the CASUALTY whose loss constitutes the syndrome.

In obsessive-compulsive disorder, OFC hyperactivity within the orbitofronto-striatal loop is one of psychiatry's most replicated circuit findings, a MARKER and plausible CAUSE that normalizes with successful treatment. Subgenual area 25 hyperactivity in depression is a MARKER and the rationale for deep brain stimulation there. In addiction, blunted OFC and VMPFC valuation is a MARKER and, on the dominant account, a partial CAUSE of compulsive choice.

ENTRY: 2.1.9 ANTERIOR CINGULATE CORTEX (ACC, BRODMANN AREAS 24, 32, 33)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The ACC arises from the medial wall of the dorsal telencephalon, on the border where neocortex grades into older mesocortex; area 33 retains that transitional character. It myelinates in Flechsig's limbic early wave yet its function matures slowly, with conflict monitoring and error processing developing through adolescence.

Its distinctive cellular asset, the von Economo neurons shared with the anterior insula, appear late in gestation and reach adult numbers only in the first years of life (with most counts from human and ape material), making them among the latest-arriving neurons in the cortex.

No sharply dated critical period is established for the ACC; its affective circuitry appears broadly experience-sensitive across early life.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The ACC shares with the anterior insula the primary seat of behavioural-variant frontotemporal dementia, whose earliest and most selective loss is precisely the von Economo neurons; per that disorder it is a CASUALTY, and the von Economo population is its named selective vulnerability.

In obsessive-compulsive disorder and in chronic pain, ACC hyperactivity is a MARKER and a plausible partial CAUSE; cingulotomy, the surgical lesioning of area 24, relieves both in refractory cases, which is causal evidence of an unusual directness.

In depression, dorsal ACC and subgenual changes are MARKERS with treatment-predictive value. Akinetic mutism after bilateral ACC destruction shows the region as a pure CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.1.10 POSTERIOR CINGULATE CORTEX (PCC) AND PRECUNEUS, BRODMANN AREAS 23, 31, 7m

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The PCC and precuneus arise from the caudal medial wall of the dorsal telencephalon. They myelinate in the limbic and paralimbic early-intermediate wave, but their signature property, hub status in the default mode network, is assembled slowly: long-range functional connectivity between PCC and medial prefrontal cortex is weak in infancy and reaches adult form across childhood and adolescence.

The region's extreme resting metabolic rate is a lifelong trait, present once the network consolidates. No discrete critical period is established here. It matures after the sensory cortices whose information it integrates and roughly alongside the other association hubs.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The PCC and precuneus are the anatomical bullseye of Alzheimer disease: amyloid deposition begins preferentially here and across the default mode network, and PCC hypometabolism on fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography (FDG-PET) is among the earliest imaging signs, present before symptoms. Per that disorder the region is both an early MARKER and a progressive CASUALTY; the leading mechanistic account ties its vulnerability to its lifelong metabolic and connectional load, activity-dependent amyloid release taxing the busiest hub hardest.

No other disorder claims it as a primary seat. Precuneus atrophy is a MARKER of early-onset Alzheimer variants, and PCC dysfunction is a MARKER in traumatic brain injury and disorders of consciousness, where its connectivity tracks the level of awareness.

ENTRY: 2.1.11 THE DEFAULT MODE NETWORK CORE TRIAD (PUC)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The triad's three nodes are cortical tissue of the dorsal telencephalon, but the object itself is a wiring achievement: in the neonate the three regions are only weakly coupled, fragmentary default mode organization appears in infancy, and the adult-form triad with strong medial prefrontal to posterior cingulate coupling consolidates across childhood and adolescence as the cingulum bundle and long association fibres myelinate.

The triad therefore matures later than any of its members' local circuitry, on the schedule of the slowest cable, into the third decade. Its emergence tracks the development of autobiographical memory and mental time travel in the child. No critical period is established for a network-level entity of this kind, and none can honestly be stated.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The triad is the preferential territory of Alzheimer disease: amyloid maps onto the default mode network with striking fidelity, mPFC and PCC first, and per that disorder the triad is an early MARKER and progressive CASUALTY, with its high lifelong activity the proposed mechanism of vulnerability.

In major depression, failure to deactivate the triad during tasks and its hyperconnectivity with the subgenual cingulate are replicated MARKERS tied to rumination, with a debated causal share.

Altered triad connectivity is a MARKER in schizophrenia, autism, and disorders of consciousness. No disorder has the triad as its initiating seat in the strict CAUSE sense.

ENTRY: 2.1.12 INSULAR CORTEX (THE INSULA)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The insula differentiates early from the ventrolateral wall of the dorsal telencephalon and is one of the first cortical territories to fold: it is visible as a distinct field by midgestation, and the frontal, temporal, and parietal opercula grow over it during the third trimester, a process (opercularization) not fully complete at birth.

Its posterior granular sector matures on a sensory-cortex schedule; the anterior agranular sector, home of the von Economo neurons that arrive late in gestation and accumulate postnatally, matures on an association-cortex schedule into adolescence. Interoceptive predictive machinery is presumed to tune across early life, but no dated critical period is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The anterior insula, with the anterior cingulate, is the primary seat of behavioural-variant frontotemporal dementia, with selective early loss of von Economo neurons; per that disorder it is a CASUALTY and the von Economo population is its selective vulnerability.

In addiction the insula is closer to a CAUSE: strokes destroying it can abruptly abolish cigarette craving, the most direct causal evidence any cortical region has in addiction.

Anterior insula hyperreactivity is a MARKER, and plausibly a partial CAUSE, in anxiety disorders and panic; altered insular interoception is a MARKER in depression, eating disorders, and functional pain syndromes. Insular seizures mimic panic and are a CAUSE in that epilepsy. The posterior insula is a CASUALTY in operculo-insular stroke, producing central pain.

ENTRY: 2.1.13 BROCA'S AREA (LEFT INFERIOR FRONTAL GYRUS, BRODMANN AREAS 44 AND 45)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: Broca's area forms in the left inferior frontal gyrus from the dorsal telencephalon, with its structural left-right asymmetry detectable in the fetus. It is a late-myelinating association territory, and its principal cable, the arcuate fasciculus, is among the slowest tracts to mature, strengthening through childhood in step with grammar.

Language acquisition gives it the best-known sensitive period in human cognition: native-like syntax and phonology are reliably achieved only with exposure in roughly the first years to puberty, a gradient rather than a wall, and late first-language exposure (as in late-signing deaf children) leaves permanent grammatical limits. It matures after the temporal language cortex whose output it sequences.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In left middle cerebral artery stroke Broca's area is the classic CASUALTY, producing Broca's aphasia.

It is the primary seat of the nonfluent-agrammatic variant of primary progressive aphasia, where tau-predominant degeneration of the left inferior frontal and insular cortex constitutes the disease; a CASUALTY whose loss is the syndrome.

In developmental stuttering, structural and timing anomalies of the left inferior frontal cortex and its dorsal tracts are the most replicated findings, and the region is a plausible partial CAUSE. Inferior frontal dysfunction is a MARKER in nonfluent presentations of frontotemporal dementia and in some developmental language disorder cohorts.

ENTRY: 2.1.14 WERNICKE'S AREA (LEFT POSTERIOR SUPERIOR TEMPORAL GYRUS, BRODMANN AREA 22)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: Wernicke's territory forms on the left posterior superior temporal gyrus from the dorsal telencephalon, adjacent to the planum temporale whose leftward asymmetry is present by the third trimester, one of the earliest structural asymmetries in the brain. As auditory association cortex it myelinates in the intermediate wave, after A1 and before the frontal language cortex.

Its sensitive period is the receptive half of the language window: phoneme discrimination narrows to the native inventory between roughly 6 and 12 months, the earliest dated milestone of the language critical period, and comprehension circuitry matures ahead of the production circuitry in Broca's area, mirroring the child's comprehension-first course.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In posterior left middle cerebral artery stroke the region is the classic CASUALTY, producing Wernicke's aphasia.

In the logopenic variant of primary progressive aphasia, degeneration seated in the left posterior temporal and inferior parietal cortex, usually with Alzheimer pathology, makes this territory the primary seat and CASUALTY.

In schizophrenia, structural deficits of the left superior temporal gyrus are among the most replicated findings and activation here accompanies auditory verbal hallucinations; a MARKER, with a contested causal share in the hallucinated voice. Left temporal language cortex dysfunction is also a MARKER in developmental language disorder.

ENTRY: 2.1.15 THE MEDIAL TEMPORAL INTERFACE CORTICES: ENTORHINAL, PERIRHINAL, AND PARAHIPPOCAMPAL

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: These cortices arise from the caudomedial pallium at the boundary where six-laminae neocortex grades into the three-laminae allocortex of the hippocampus; the entorhinal cortex differentiates strikingly early, with its cellular islands recognizable in the first half of gestation, among the earliest cortex to acquire adult-like organization.

Myelination follows the limbic early wave, but the memory functions served mature slowly: episodic memory emerges across early childhood as these interfaces and their hippocampal partner consolidate, and infantile amnesia marks the immature period. In rodents, grid cells in the medial entorhinal cortex appear in the weeks after eye opening, later than hippocampal place cells (rodent data); the human timetable is not established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The entorhinal and transentorhinal cortex is where Alzheimer disease begins: Braak stage I tau pathology starts here, and its layer II island neurons are among the most selectively vulnerable cells in the brain, degenerating decades before diagnosis. Per Alzheimer disease the region is the seat of earliest damage, an early MARKER and first CASUALTY, and on the spreading-tau account a proximal CAUSE of the propagation to hippocampus and neocortex.

In temporal lobe epilepsy the entorhinal cortex is part of the seizure-generating circuit, a CO-CAUSE with the hippocampus.

Perirhinal degeneration contributes to the semantic and object-recognition loss of semantic dementia, where it is a CASUALTY at the margin of the anterior temporal seat. Parahippocampal atrophy is an imaging MARKER of early Alzheimer disease.

ENTRY: 2.1.16 RETROSPLENIAL CORTEX (BRODMANN AREAS 29 AND 30)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The retrosplenial cortex forms on the caudal medial wall behind the splenium, transitional tissue of the dorsal telencephalon between neocortex and the cingulate mesocortex, myelinating in the limbic early-intermediate wave.

Its function depends on the Papez-circuit partners it links, and its navigational role matures with them across childhood; head-direction processing, its rodent signature, appears very early in the rat pup, with head-direction cells functional around eye opening (rodent data). No dated human critical period is established for this region.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The retrosplenial and posterior cingulate territory shows among the earliest hypometabolism in Alzheimer disease and its prodrome, making it an early MARKER and CASUALTY; its position as the relay between hippocampus and cortical memory systems is the proposed reason its dysfunction is so symptomatic so early.

Focal retrosplenial damage, classically right-sided, produces topographical disorientation with landmark recognition intact; a pure CASUALTY syndrome.

No disorder has the retrosplenial cortex as its initiating primary seat; per every named disease it is casualty or marker, never cause.

ENTRY: 2.1.17 POSTERIOR PARIETAL CORTEX (BRODMANN AREAS 5, 7, 39, 40)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The posterior parietal cortex arises from the dorsal telencephalon between the somatosensory and visual fields it will integrate, and belongs with prefrontal cortex to Flechsig's terminal zones: it myelinates late and prunes through adolescence, with parietal grey matter peaking around puberty and thinning thereafter along the same long tail as frontal cortex.

Multisensory integration and visually guided reaching mature across infancy and childhood as dorsal-stream input consolidates; number and spatial skills hosted here develop with schooling. It matures after S1 and the visual areas that feed it and roughly in step with its prefrontal partners.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In right middle cerebral artery stroke the region is the CASUALTY whose loss is hemispatial neglect; bilateral parieto-occipital damage produces Balint syndrome.

The parietal association cortex is core territory of Alzheimer disease, an early site of atrophy and hypometabolism, especially in posterior cortical atrophy and early-onset variants; a CASUALTY and imaging MARKER.

Left angular and supramarginal damage produces Gerstmann syndrome, apraxia, and alexia with agraphia; CASUALTY per each. Parietal dysfunction is a MARKER in developmental dyscalculia, with intraparietal number circuitry a plausible partial CAUSE. Its vulnerability profile is that of a late-maturing, high-connectivity hub attacked by the degenerations that favour such hubs.

ENTRY: 2.1.18 TEMPORAL ASSOCIATION CORTEX AND THE INFEROTEMPORAL CORTEX

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The inferotemporal and temporal association cortex arises from the ventrolateral dorsal telencephalon and is a Flechsig terminal territory, myelinating late and maturing through adolescence.

Its recognition machinery assembles hierarchically after the early visual areas: face-selective and category-selective patches are present in infancy in rudimentary form and are then sculpted by years of visual experience, with face expertise developing into adolescence. Early visual deprivation (congenital cataract) leaves lasting configural face-processing deficits, evidence of a sensitive period in the first months to years, though its boundaries are not precisely dated. Semantic knowledge stored here accumulates lifelong.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The anterior temporal cortex is the primary seat of semantic dementia (the semantic variant of primary progressive aphasia), usually with TDP-43 pathology; the region is the CASUALTY whose asymmetric loss is the syndrome, left-predominant for words, right-predominant for faces.

Fusiform damage produces acquired prosopagnosia (CASUALTY); congenital prosopagnosia implicates the same territory developmentally as a plausible CAUSE.

Herpes simplex encephalitis has a striking tropism for this cortex, a CASUALTY with selective vulnerability by viral route rather than cell type. Inferotemporal atrophy is a MARKER and later CASUALTY in Alzheimer disease, and fusiform hypoactivation to faces is a replicated MARKER in autism.

ENTRY: 2.1.19 FRONTAL EYE FIELDS (FEF, BRODMANN AREA 8)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The FEF forms in the caudal prefrontal territory of the dorsal telencephalon, at the junction of Flechsig's intermediate and terminal zones, maturing after the parietal eye fields that feed it salience and before full prefrontal maturity.

Its behavioural signature dates its maturation: reflexive saccades work in infancy on subcortical and parietal machinery, but voluntary saccade control, and especially the antisaccade task requiring suppression of a reflexive glance, improves steadily through childhood and reaches adult performance only in mid-adolescence, tracking FEF and prefrontal circuit maturation. No discrete critical period is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the FEF as its primary seat, and that should be said plainly.

Acute destruction, as in stroke, makes it a CASUALTY, producing transient gaze deviation toward the lesion and contralateral saccadic neglect, usually compensated within weeks by the superior colliculus.

Antisaccade errors, reflecting FEF and prefrontal control failure, are among the most reliable oculomotor MARKERS in schizophrenia, found also in unaffected relatives, hence an endophenotype; the region indexes the disease rather than initiating it. Oculomotor findings in ADHD and Parkinson disease likewise use the FEF as a MARKER of control and of basal ganglia input respectively.

ENTRY: 2.1.20 SUPRAMARGINAL GYRUS (SMG, BRODMANN AREA 40)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The SMG forms in the inferior parietal lobule from the dorsal telencephalon; the inferior parietal cortex is among the last territories to complete gyrification prenatally and belongs to Flechsig's terminal zones, maturing through adolescence.

Its phonological functions assemble with literacy and language across childhood, on the timetable of the dorsal language stream and the arcuate fasciculus; its praxis functions mature with skilled tool use over the same years. No dated critical period specific to the SMG is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the SMG as its unique primary seat.

Left SMG and adjacent white matter damage produces conduction aphasia and is central to ideomotor apraxia; per both stroke syndromes the region is a CASUALTY. Right SMG damage contributes to hemispatial neglect and to disturbed body ownership, again a CASUALTY.

Reduced left SMG activation and grey matter are replicated MARKERS in developmental dyslexia's phonological deficit, with a plausible partial causal share that remains unproven. Inferior parietal atrophy including SMG is a MARKER in logopenic progressive aphasia and Alzheimer disease.

ENTRY: 2.1.21 TEMPOROPARIETAL JUNCTION (TPJ)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The TPJ is late-assembling association cortex of the dorsal telencephalon at the meeting of temporal, parietal, and occipital fields, within Flechsig's terminal wave.

Its social function has one of the best-dated cognitive milestones in development: explicit false-belief understanding emerges around age 4, and the TPJ's selectivity for thinking about others' thoughts sharpens through later childhood and adolescence as the region specializes. Its attention-reorienting function matures earlier, on the ventral attention network's schedule. Whether early social deprivation imposes a strict sensitive period here is not established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the TPJ as its unique primary seat.

Right TPJ damage is one of the strongest lesion correlates of hemispatial neglect, a CASUALTY. Electrical interference and seizures at the TPJ can produce out-of-body experiences, making it the CAUSE of that specific phenomenon.

Atypical TPJ activation during mentalizing is one of the most consistent functional MARKERS in autism spectrum condition, with an unresolved causal share. TPJ and superior temporal involvement also appears as a MARKER in schizophrenia, where misattribution of agency is a candidate mechanism for passivity symptoms.

ENTRY: 2.1.22 SUPERIOR TEMPORAL SULCUS (STS)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The STS forms in the lateral temporal dorsal telencephalon; the sulcus itself appears around midgestation, but its cortex is terminal-zone tissue that myelinates late and keeps maturing through adolescence.

Its social tuning starts early and sharpens long: newborns prefer biological motion, voice-selective temporal responses are demonstrable in infancy, and STS selectivity for faces in motion, gaze, and voice is progressively refined across childhood. Early auditory and social experience shapes it, and the first year's perceptual narrowing (for faces and speech sounds) marks a sensitive period whose STS-specific boundaries are not precisely dated.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the STS as its unique primary seat.

The STS is the most consistently implicated cortical territory in autism spectrum condition: hypoactivation to voices, gaze, and biological motion, and structural differences, are replicated MARKERS, and because social perception is the entry point to social learning the region is a plausible partial CAUSE; that causal reading remains a hypothesis.

In schizophrenia, STS involvement accompanies auditory hallucinations and misperceived intentions, a MARKER. In stroke and in semantic dementia's spread the STS is a CASUALTY, degrading voice and face-motion perception.

ENTRY: 2.1.23 MIDDLE TEMPORAL GYRUS (MTG, BRODMANN AREA 21)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The MTG arises from the lateral temporal dorsal telencephalon in Flechsig's terminal wave; lateral temporal cortex is among the last to myelinate and continues structural maturation into the third decade.

Its lexical-semantic function accumulates for a lifetime: vocabulary has no closing window, which makes the MTG the counterexample to the critical-period pattern of the grammar circuits, and semantic knowledge here is the cognitive faculty most resistant to ageing. It matures after the superior temporal auditory areas that feed it.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the MTG as its unique primary seat.

In left middle cerebral artery stroke it is a CASUALTY central to comprehension deficits and semantic paraphasias in Wernicke-type and transcortical sensory aphasia.

In semantic dementia the degeneration seated in the anterior temporal lobe spreads through the MTG, a CASUALTY whose loss dissolves word meaning. Lateral temporal atrophy including MTG is a MARKER and later CASUALTY in Alzheimer disease.

Reduced MTG activation and connectivity is a replicated MARKER in developmental language disorder and in schizophrenia's semantic disorganization, causal share unknown.

ENTRY: 2.1.24 FRONTOPOLAR / ANTERIOR PREFRONTAL CORTEX (BRODMANN AREA 10)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: Area 10, the most anterior cortex, arises from the rostral pole of the dorsal telencephalon and is by most measures the last cortical territory to mature: a Flechsig terminal zone, disproportionately expanded in humans, with unusually low neuronal density and unusually high dendritic spine counts, and structural maturation running through adolescence into the third decade with no sharp endpoint.

Human layer III pyramidal neurons in prefrontal cortex show protracted spine remodelling, extending across decades in post-mortem material. The faculties it hosts, prospective memory, multitasking, and metacognition, are among the last cognitive abilities to reach adult levels, in late adolescence. No dated critical period is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has area 10 as its primary seat, and this should be stated plainly: it is remarkable for how seldom focal disease strikes it.

In frontotemporal dementia the frontal pole is engulfed as the degeneration advances, a CASUALTY. Traumatic brain injury frequently damages it with the orbital cortex, a mechanical CASUALTY producing planning and multitasking failure.

Frontopolar dysfunction, particularly of metacognition and reality monitoring, is a MARKER in schizophrenia, and altered area 10 activity appears as a MARKER in depression's rumination and in gambling and exploration abnormalities; in none is a causal seat established.

ENTRY: 2.1.25 PRE-SUPPLEMENTARY MOTOR AREA (PRE-SMA, ROSTRAL MEDIAL BRODMANN AREA 6)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The pre-SMA forms on the rostral medial wall of area 6, from the dorsal telencephalon, and matures on a prefrontal rather than a motor schedule: it is the cognitive edge of the motor hierarchy, connected to prefrontal cortex and striatum rather than to the spinal cord, and its myelination and pruning follow the late association-cortex wave.

Its signature capacity, stopping and switching actions, matures through childhood and adolescence, with response inhibition reaching adult levels in the late teens. It matures after the SMA proper and M1, reversing the caudal-first gradient of the motor strip. No dated critical period is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the pre-SMA as its unique primary seat.

In Tourette syndrome, premonitory urges and tic generation engage the pre-SMA and SMA, and stimulation here can evoke urge-like sensations; the region is a plausible partial CAUSE within the cortico-striatal loop, with the primary fault usually placed in the striatum.

In stuttering, altered pre-SMA and basal ganglia timing signals are a replicated MARKER with a debated causal share. In Parkinson disease its hypoactivation during self-initiated action marks it a CASUALTY of failed pallido-thalamic input. Seizures and lesions here impair action initiation and produce motor arrest; per that epilepsy it is the CAUSE.

ENTRY: 2.1.26 SUPPLEMENTARY EYE FIELD (SEF, MEDIAL BRODMANN AREA 6/8)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The SEF forms on the medial frontal wall at the 6/8 border of the dorsal telencephalon, within the late-maturing association wave, after the FEF-parietal circuitry whose output it supervises.

Its functions, internally sequenced saccades and the performance monitoring of gaze, mature with the executive system through adolescence. The region is thinly studied developmentally, and this entry is short for that reason: no human myelination date, no critical period, and no maturational milestone specific to the SEF can be stated honestly. Longitudinal imaging with oculomotor tasks would settle its timetable.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the SEF as its primary seat, and clinical lesions confined to it are rare enough that its human casualty syndrome is only partially characterized: reported deficits involve saccade sequences and self-timed gaze rather than any elementary loss.

Abnormal sequential and self-paced saccades serve as a MARKER of medial frontal dysfunction in Parkinson disease, progressive supranuclear palsy, and schizophrenia, without the SEF being the seat of any of them.

Per stroke and tumour encroaching on the medial 6/8 wall it is an incidental CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.1.27 SUPERIOR PARIETAL LOBULE (SPL, BRODMANN AREAS 5 AND 7)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The SPL arises from the dorsal telencephalon behind the postcentral sulcus; area 5 matures on a near-sensory schedule after S1, while area 7 belongs to the late terminal wave, so the lobule spans the maturational gradient it sits on.

Visually guided reaching appears in the first half-year of life and is refined for years; coordinate transformation and spatial working memory mature through childhood and adolescence with the dorsal stream. Parietal grey matter peaks around puberty and thins into adulthood. No dated critical period is established for the SPL itself.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the SPL as its unique primary seat.

Its classical casualty syndrome is optic ataxia, misreaching under visual guidance after superior parietal and intraparietal damage, and with bilateral lesions the Balint triad; per stroke it is a CASUALTY.

The SPL and adjacent intraparietal cortex are early territory of posterior cortical atrophy and of parietal-predominant Alzheimer presentations, a CASUALTY and imaging MARKER. Dorsal-stream and SPL dysfunction is a replicated MARKER in developmental coordination disorder, with an unproven causal share.

ENTRY: 2.1.28 PARAHIPPOCAMPAL PLACE AREA (PPA)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The PPA is a functionally defined patch of ventral temporal cortex, dorsal telencephalic tissue on the parahippocampal gyrus, identified by its scene response rather than by cytoarchitecture. Scene-selective responses are present in infancy in rudimentary form, and the patch's selectivity and size increase through childhood into adolescence, maturing later than the face patches by some reports, with the category map's basic layout in place early and its tuning sharpened by years of visual experience.

Whether scene selectivity requires visual experience at all is informatively answered by congenitally blind subjects, whose parahippocampal territory responds to scene-like layout in touch and sound; there is no evidence for a strict visual critical period here.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the PPA as its primary seat.

Right parahippocampal damage, typically posterior cerebral artery stroke, produces landmark agnosia, the inability to recognize places; per that syndrome the PPA is a CASUALTY.

In developmental topographical disorientation, lifelong inability to form cognitive maps without any lesion, altered PPA and retrosplenial connectivity is the leading finding, a MARKER and candidate CAUSE in a condition still being characterized. Parahippocampal atrophy in Alzheimer disease degrades scene memory, the PPA a CASUALTY within the wider medial temporal collapse.

ENTRY: 2.1.29 VISUAL WORD FORM AREA (VWFA, LEFT OCCIPITOTEMPORAL FUSIFORM)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The VWFA is the cortex's newest acquisition: writing is roughly five thousand years old, far too recent for evolution, so the region is assembled anew in every reader by cultural recycling of ventral visual cortex, emerging in the left occipitotemporal fusiform within months of reading instruction, typically around ages 5 to 7, at a site whose connectivity to the language network predates and predicts it.

Adults who learn to read late still develop VWFA responses, so there is no strict critical period, though childhood learning yields stronger automatization. It is the one cortical region whose very existence is experience-dependent, and it matures, by definition, after the language and visual systems it joins.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The VWFA is the seat of the reading disorders. Acquired damage, classically left posterior cerebral artery stroke or surgical resection, produces pure alexia, reading letter by letter with writing intact; a CASUALTY.

In developmental dyslexia, hypoactivation of the VWFA and its dorsal partners is the most replicated functional finding; it is a reliable MARKER, and because the region fails to specialize rather than being destroyed, the deeper fault is usually placed upstream in phonological circuitry, leaving the VWFA a MARKER and disputed partial CAUSE.

No degenerative disease selects it; in posterior cortical atrophy and logopenic aphasia reading loss marks it a CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.1.30 THE LATERAL OCCIPITAL COMPLEX AND EXTRASTRIATE BODY AREA (LOC, EBA)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The LOC and EBA are functionally defined patches of lateral occipitotemporal cortex from the dorsal telencephalon, downstream of the early visual areas and maturing after them. Object-selective LOC responses are measurable in infancy and reach adult-like selectivity across childhood; body-selective EBA responses likewise emerge early and sharpen with experience into adolescence.

Sight restored after congenital blindness leaves object recognition measurably abnormal despite years of vision, evidence that early visual experience matters for this territory, though a sharply dated critical period for LOC or EBA specifically has not been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the LOC or EBA as its primary seat.

Damage to the lateral occipital territory produces visual object agnosia, the inability to recognize objects by sight with elementary vision intact, the classic case being carbon monoxide poisoning, to which this cortex is notably vulnerable; per those injuries it is a CASUALTY, and hypoxic sensitivity is its named vulnerability.

Altered EBA responses to bodies are reported as MARKERS in anorexia nervosa and body image disturbance, findings suggestive but not settled. In posterior cortical atrophy the complex is a CASUALTY within the ventral-stream collapse.

ENTRY: 2.1.31 SECONDARY SOMATOSENSORY CORTEX (S2, PARIETAL OPERCULUM)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: S2 forms on the parietal operculum, dorsal telencephalic cortex that roofs the insula during the third-trimester opercularization. It matures after S1, in the unimodal association wave: somatosensory evoked responses in S2 are recordable in infancy but its integrative and bilateral functions refine through childhood.

Notably, S2 receives its own thalamic input in parallel with S1 rather than only serially after it, so its assembly is not purely downstream. Developmental study of human S2 is thin; no myelination date or critical period specific to it can be stated honestly, and this entry is short for that reason.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has S2 as its unique primary seat.

Operculo-insular stroke involving S2 produces tactile agnosia and, distinctively, pseudothalamic central pain; per those syndromes it is a CASUALTY. Because the parietal operculum sits deep in the middle cerebral artery territory it is a frequent silent casualty of large strokes.

Opercular seizures produce somatosensory auras and salivation; per that epilepsy the region is the CAUSE. Altered S2 responses appear as MARKERS in chronic pain conditions and in functional sensory disorders, causal share unknown.

ENTRY: 2.1.32 THE AUDITORY BELT AND PARABELT (BRODMANN AREA 42 AND BEYOND)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The belt and parabelt form on the superior temporal plane around A1, from the dorsal telencephalon, and mature strictly after their core: myelination and synaptic maturation proceed outward from A1 in the unimodal association wave across infancy and childhood, the auditory system's own copy of the primary-first gradient.

Their sensitive period is the speech-sound window: perceptual narrowing to native phoneme categories between roughly 6 and 12 months of age is implemented in this tissue and its superior temporal neighbours, and late cochlear implantation shows that higher-order auditory cortex is the level that suffers most from early deprivation, with cross-modal takeover by vision.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the belt and parabelt as its unique primary seat.

Bilateral damage sparing A1 produces auditory agnosia, and right-lateralized damage produces acquired amusia, the loss of music perception; per both the region is a CASUALTY. Congenital amusia implicates right fronto-temporal connectivity including the parabelt as a MARKER and candidate CAUSE.

In schizophrenia, shrinkage of superior temporal auditory association cortex is among the most replicated structural findings and tracks hallucination severity, a MARKER with contested causal weight. In presbycusis and after deafness the belt is a CASUALTY of deprivation-driven reorganization.

ENTRY: 2.1.33 THE INSULA, ANTERIOR AND POSTERIOR DIVISIONS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The two divisions of the insula develop on visibly different schedules from the same early-folding insular anlage of the dorsal telencephalon. The posterior granular division matures like sensory cortex, its interoceptive and pain maps functional in infancy, when nociceptive cortical responses are already recordable.

The anterior agranular division matures like prefrontal cortex: it is where the von Economo neurons accumulate through early childhood, and its functions of interoceptive awareness, subjective feeling, and salience switching mature through adolescence. The posterior-to-anterior maturation of the insula is therefore a compressed replica of the whole cortical gradient. No sharply dated critical period is established for either division.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The anterior division, with the anterior cingulate, is the primary seat of behavioural-variant frontotemporal dementia, its von Economo neurons the first and most selective loss; per that disorder a CASUALTY with a named cellular vulnerability.

Anterior insula hyperreactivity is a MARKER and plausible partial CAUSE in anxiety and panic disorder; its damage can abolish addictive craving, causal evidence per addiction.

The posterior division is the seat of operculo-insular central pain after stroke (CASUALTY) and of painful insular seizures (CAUSE per that epilepsy). Altered posterior-to-anterior interoceptive signalling is a MARKER across depression, eating disorders, and functional somatic syndromes.

ENTRY: 2.2 THE SUBCORTICAL TELENCEPHALON

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The subcortical telencephalon arises mainly from the subpallium, the ventral division of the telencephalic vesicle, which is visible in the human embryo by around week 5 of gestation.

Its progenitor territory is organized into the lateral ganglionic eminence (LGE), the medial ganglionic eminence (MGE), the caudal ganglionic eminence (CGE), and the preoptic area, each defined by its own transcription factor code (GSX2 for the LGE, NKX2-1 for the MGE among others).

The LGE generates the striatal projection neurons, the MGE generates the pallidum and most cortical and striatal interneurons, and the CGE contributes further interneuron classes; the hippocampal formation, by contrast, is pallial, arising from the medial pallium at the cortical hem.

Neurogenesis in these territories is largely prenatal in the human, with the ganglionic eminences at their peak in the second trimester and regressing before birth, but interneuron migration into human frontal cortex continues into the early postnatal months.

Circuit maturation is protracted: striatal dopamine innervation, hippocampal wiring, and septal cholinergic projections all continue to refine across childhood and adolescence.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: As a division, the subcortical telencephalon is the seat of several of the great regional disorders: Huntington disease in the striatum, temporal lobe epilepsy and Alzheimer-type amnesia in the hippocampal formation, and the cholinergic loss of Alzheimer disease in the basal forebrain; each is treated under its own entry.

Developmental failure of the subpallium produces interneuron deficits that are repeatedly implicated in epilepsy, autism spectrum disorder, and schizophrenia, where the subpallial derivatives act as a CAUSE at the circuit level.

Holoprosencephaly, in which the ventral forebrain fails to divide, damages this territory earliest and most severely; there the region is a CASUALTY of a patterning failure upstream of it.

ENTRY: 2.2.1 THE HIPPOCAMPAL FORMATION (DENTATE GYRUS, CA3, CA2, CA1, SUBICULUM)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The hippocampal formation arises from the medial pallium, the most medial strip of the dorsal telencephalon, patterned by the adjacent cortical hem, a WNT and BMP (bone morphogenetic protein) signalling source; hem ablation in mouse deletes the hippocampus.

The Ammon's horn fields are generated prenatally, but the dentate gyrus is a famous exception: its granule cells are born late and largely postnatally, with a displaced germinal matrix that migrates into the forming blade.

In the rat (rodent data), roughly 85 percent of dentate granule cells are generated after birth, peaking at the end of the first postnatal week; in the human the granule cell layer is still immature at birth and grows substantially over the first postnatal months.

Whether the dentate gyrus keeps adding neurons in the adult human is openly contested: Sorrells and colleagues (2018) found essentially no new neurons after childhood, while Boldrini (2018) and Moreno-Jimenez (2019) reported persistent adult neurogenesis; methodological differences, including tissue fixation, are the leading explanation, and the question is reported here as unresolved.

The trisynaptic circuit wires up in a fixed order, and hippocampus-dependent memory matures slowly across early childhood, which is one candidate explanation for infantile amnesia.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In Alzheimer disease the hippocampal formation, together with entorhinal cortex, is among the earliest sites of tau tangle pathology and neuron loss, producing the signature amnestic onset; per disorder it is a CASUALTY of a spreading process and its atrophy on imaging is a MARKER used in diagnosis.

In mesial temporal lobe epilepsy with hippocampal sclerosis, CA1 and the hilus lose neurons while the dentate granule cells sprout aberrant recurrent mossy fibres; the sclerotic hippocampus is both a CASUALTY of the initial insult and a CAUSE of the ongoing seizures, which is why its surgical removal can cure the epilepsy.

The CA1 field is selectively vulnerable to global ischaemia and hypoxia, dying after transient cardiac arrest while neighbouring fields survive; in transient global amnesia and hypoxic amnesia the hippocampus is the CASUALTY.

Hippocampal volume loss is also a reproducible finding in major depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, and chronic stress states, where the region reads as a CASUALTY of glucocorticoid exposure and, on imaging, a MARKER; in schizophrenia anterior hippocampal hyperactivity is a candidate CAUSE of psychosis in some models, though this remains a model rather than a settled fact.

ENTRY: 2.2.1.1 PRESUBICULUM

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The presubiculum is a transitional periarchicortical field of the medial pallium, generated prenatally alongside the subiculum and entorhinal cortex within the hippocampal formation's medial pallial territory.

Its defining physiological population, the head direction cells, comes online early: in the rat pup (rodent data) head direction tuning is already present at eye opening around postnatal day 12 to 14, among the earliest spatial signals in the brain, preceding stable place and grid signals.

A precise human maturation timetable for the presubiculum itself has not been established, and no honest timescale beyond the general prenatal genesis of periarchicortex can be stated; developmental imaging with laminar resolution would be needed to settle it.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The presubiculum has an unusual distinction in Alzheimer disease: it develops characteristic lake-like diffuse amyloid deposits, and as a component of the medial temporal memory circuit it degenerates with its neighbours; it is a CASUALTY there, and no disorder has the presubiculum as its primary seat.

Damage to the presubicular head direction system is one proposed substrate of the spatial disorientation and wandering of Alzheimer disease, but this attribution is inferential rather than demonstrated in humans.

ENTRY: 2.2.1.2 PARASUBICULUM

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The parasubiculum is the narrowest strip of the periarchicortical transition, arising prenatally from the same medial pallial territory as the presubiculum and entorhinal cortex.

It is far less studied than its neighbours; its cell birthdates and maturation have been characterized mainly in rodents as part of general hippocampal formation development, and no dedicated human timetable exists.

In the rodent it contains grid, border, and head direction cells and strong theta rhythmicity, properties that emerge over the first postnatal weeks with the rest of the entorhinal spatial system (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the parasubiculum as its primary seat.

It shares the medial temporal lobe's fate in Alzheimer disease and temporal lobe epilepsy as a CASUALTY, and epileptiform activity has been recorded in parahippocampal transition zones in human tissue, but a distinct parasubicular clinical syndrome has never been described.

ENTRY: 2.2.2 THE AMYGDALA (LATERAL, BASAL, ACCESSORY BASAL, CENTRAL, MEDIAL, AND CORTICAL NUCLEI)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The amygdala is a developmental composite, which is why its nuclei behave so differently: the lateral, basal, and accessory basal nuclei are pallial, cortex-like glutamatergic structures, while the central and medial nuclei are subpallial, striatum-like and pallidum-like GABAergic (gamma-aminobutyric acid) structures drawing on the ganglionic eminences and preoptic area.

Its principal nuclei are identifiable in the human foetus during the first half of gestation, and the amygdala is functional early: it responds to emotional faces in infancy.

It matures ahead of the prefrontal cortex that regulates it, and this timing gap across adolescence is a standard explanation for adolescent emotional reactivity.

Rodent work (rodent data) defines a sensitive period for fear learning: before about postnatal day 10 rat pups show attachment learning rather than amygdala-dependent fear conditioning, which then switches on; early adversity shifts this timetable earlier.

Human amygdala volume increases through childhood, and early institutional deprivation is associated with altered amygdala volume and earlier-maturing amygdala-prefrontal connectivity, evidence of a human sensitive period for emotional circuitry.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In anxiety disorders and post-traumatic stress disorder the amygdala, especially the central nucleus output and its lateral input, is hyperresponsive; in circuit terms it is the closest thing these disorders have to a CAUSE, though the initiating lesion is distributed.

In Urbach-Wiethe disease, bilateral calcification selectively destroys the amygdala and abolishes fear recognition and fear experience, the famous patient SM; the amygdala there is the CASUALTY and the deficit maps its function.

Temporal lobe epilepsy frequently involves the amygdala alongside the hippocampus; sclerotic amygdala tissue is both CASUALTY and, when it seeds seizures, CAUSE.

Amygdala enlargement in early childhood is a reported MARKER in autism spectrum disorder, and amygdala reactivity is a MARKER in depression; in Klüver-Bucy syndrome bilateral amygdala and temporal damage is the CAUSE of the placidity and hyperorality.

ENTRY: 2.2.3 THE BED NUCLEUS OF THE STRIA TERMINALIS (BNST)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The bed nucleus of the stria terminalis (BNST) is a subpallial structure of the extended amygdala, assembled from multiple embryonic sources: its various subnuclei draw on the lateral and medial ganglionic eminences and the preoptic area, mirroring the mixed striatal and pallidal character of its cell types.

Its principal cell groups are in place prenatally, but the BNST is a lifelong construction site in one specific sense: it is among the most sexually dimorphic structures in the brain.

The principal nucleus of the human BNST (the darkly staining posteromedial subdivision) is larger in males, and this dimorphism emerges surprisingly late, reaching adult male-female difference only around puberty into adulthood in human post-mortem series.

Rodent data show the dimorphism is organized by perinatal gonadal hormones acting on cell survival (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The BNST is the leading circuit candidate for sustained anxiety as opposed to phasic fear: human and rodent work assigns prolonged threat monitoring to the BNST and acute threat to the central amygdala, making the BNST a putative CAUSE-level node in generalized anxiety disorder and in stress-induced relapse to drug seeking.

Post-mortem studies reported that BNST volume and neuron characteristics in transgender women resembled those of control women; this is a MARKER-level observation about identity, not a disorder, and its interpretation is debated because the dimorphism develops after most gender identity is established.

No degenerative disease has the BNST as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.2.4 THE BASAL GANGLIA

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The basal ganglia assemble from two ganglionic eminences of the subpallium: the lateral ganglionic eminence (LGE) supplies the striatal projection neurons, and the medial ganglionic eminence (MGE) supplies the pallidum and the striatum's interneurons, with the subthalamic nucleus arriving from a separate, hypothalamic origin and the dopamine neurons from the ventral mesencephalon.

The eminences are prominent by the late first trimester in the human, and striatal neurogenesis is concentrated in the first and second trimesters.

Assembly is sequential: the direct and indirect pathway neuron types are specified embryonically, dopamine axons innervate the striatum prenatally, and the loops through thalamus and cortex become functional before birth but myelinate and refine across childhood.

Rodent data show striosome (patch) neurons are born before matrix neurons, an inside-out compartmental sequence preserved across mammals (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The basal ganglia are the most disease-named circuit in the brain: Parkinson disease (dopamine loss into the loop), Huntington disease (striatal degeneration), dystonia, hemiballismus, Tourette syndrome, and obsessive-compulsive disorder all live here, each detailed under the responsible component's entry.

The circuit logic makes lesion location legible: too little movement points to dopamine or direct pathway failure, unwanted movement points to indirect pathway or subthalamic failure.

The basal ganglia are also selectively vulnerable as a set: carbon monoxide poisoning and hypoxia strike the globus pallidus, and kernicterus (bilirubin toxicity of the newborn) strikes the pallidum and subthalamic nucleus, producing dyskinetic cerebral palsy; in these the basal ganglia are CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.2.4.1 THE STRIATUM (CAUDATE NUCLEUS, PUTAMEN, AND NUCLEUS ACCUMBENS)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: Striatal projection neurons, the medium spiny neurons, are born in the lateral ganglionic eminence and migrate the short distance into the forming striatum, with human striatal neurogenesis concentrated in the first two trimesters.

The compartmental architecture assembles early: striosome neurons are born first and matrix neurons later (established in rodent birthdating; rodent data), and dopamine innervation arrives prenatally, initially targeting the striosomes.

Direct pathway (dopamine D1 receptor) and indirect pathway (D2 receptor) identities are specified at birth of the neuron, not learned.

Postnatally the striatum is a centre of protracted refinement: dopamine receptor density in human and primate striatum overshoots in childhood and is pruned through adolescence, a timetable often linked to adolescent reward sensitivity.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Huntington disease is the striatum's own disease: the mutant huntingtin CAG expansion kills striatal medium spiny neurons first and worst, indirect pathway neurons before direct, producing chorea then rigidity; the striatum is the prime CASUALTY of a genetic CAUSE expressed in every cell, and why these neurons are the most vulnerable in the body remains only partly explained.

In Parkinson disease the striatum is a CASUALTY of dopamine denervation from the substantia nigra, with the posterior putamen deafferented first.

In obsessive-compulsive disorder and Tourette syndrome, corticostriatal loop dysfunction is the leading circuit model, placing the striatum at CAUSE level; in Tourette, reduced striatal interneuron numbers have been reported post mortem.

In addiction the ventral striatum is where drug reinforcement is computed and the dorsal striatum is where compulsion consolidates, a CAUSE-level role; striatal dopamine transporter imaging is a diagnostic MARKER separating Parkinson disease from its mimics.

ENTRY: 2.2.4.2 THE GLOBUS PALLIDUS, EXTERNAL SEGMENT (GPe)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The external globus pallidus (GPe) derives from the medial ganglionic eminence of the subpallium, under the control of the transcription factor NKX2-1, with its projection neurons generated early in the subpallial neurogenic period, prenatally in the human.

Its two principal adult cell classes, prototypic neurons projecting to the subthalamic nucleus and arkypallidal neurons projecting back to the striatum, arise from distinct progenitor pools; this cell-type split was resolved only in the 2010s in rodents (rodent data), and its human developmental timetable is not established.

Pallidal neurons are among the most continuously active in the brain, firing tonically at high rates from early life, an energetic commitment relevant to the structure's vulnerability.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The GPe is the central switchboard of the indirect pathway, and in Parkinson disease its firing becomes abnormally patterned and burst-like; it is a CASUALTY of dopamine loss whose altered output helps transmit the disease's bradykinesia, a downstream CAUSE within the circuit.

The pallidum as a whole is selectively vulnerable to hypoxia, carbon monoxide poisoning, and kernicterus, appearing as bilateral pallidal lesions on imaging; in these it is the CASUALTY, and bilateral pallidal necrosis is a radiological MARKER of carbon monoxide exposure.

Pantothenate kinase-associated neurodegeneration deposits iron in the globus pallidus, the eye-of-the-tiger sign, a MARKER and CASUALTY; no common disorder has the GPe alone as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.2.4.3 THE SUBTHALAMIC NUCLEUS (STN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The subthalamic nucleus (STN) is, by origin, a hypothalamic structure: its neurons are generated in the caudal hypothalamic anlage near the mammillary recess and migrate dorsolaterally into their adult position against the internal capsule.

In the human embryo a subthalamic primordium is described at about 33 to 35 days of gestation, and by roughly days 44 to 51 the nucleus is identifiable near the mammillary region with cellular strands still connecting it to its presumed origin at the supramammillary recess.

It is glutamatergic, the only excitatory nucleus of the basal ganglia core, consistent with its origin among the glutamatergic posterior hypothalamic nuclei rather than the GABAergic subpallium.

Its adult firing is tonic and fast from early on; no distinct human postnatal maturation timetable or sensitive period for the STN has been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Classical hemiballismus, wild flinging movements of the contralateral limbs, follows destruction of the STN, usually by a small stroke; the STN is the CASUALTY and the syndrome is the purest lesion-to-symptom mapping in the basal ganglia.

In Parkinson disease the STN becomes overactive and abnormally synchronized in the beta band; it is a CASUALTY of upstream dopamine loss but a proximate CAUSE of bradykinesia and rigidity, which is exactly why high-frequency deep brain stimulation of the STN, disrupting that pathological output, is the most widely used surgical therapy for Parkinson disease.

STN beta-band power is used as an electrophysiological MARKER to guide adaptive stimulation; no neurodegenerative disease has the STN as its primary seat, though it is involved in progressive supranuclear palsy as a CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.2.4.4 THE GLOBUS PALLIDUS, INTERNAL SEGMENT (GPi), AND THE SUBSTANTIA NIGRA PARS RETICULATA (SNr)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The internal globus pallidus (GPi) and the substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr) are one output structure split in two by the internal capsule; the GPi is a subpallial, medial ganglionic eminence derivative, while the SNr assembles in the ventral midbrain, its GABAergic neurons generated in the embryonic midbrain and intermingling with the dopamine neurons above them.

Both are prenatal in origin in the human, and both fire tonically at high rates, holding thalamus and superior colliculus under continuous inhibition from early life; the developmental onset of this tonic mode in humans is not established.

The dual origin of one functional structure is a reminder that the basal ganglia output stage was assembled across two brain vesicles.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In Parkinson disease the output stage fires excessively and in pathological patterns; it is the final common path of the disease's motor signs, a CASUALTY of dopamine loss acting as proximate CAUSE of the poverty of movement, and GPi deep brain stimulation and pallidotomy treat both parkinsonism and dystonia by silencing it.

In dystonia, GPi output is abnormally patterned rather than simply excessive, and GPi stimulation is the standard surgical therapy, especially for genetic generalized dystonia.

The GPi shares the pallidum's selective vulnerability to carbon monoxide, hypoxia, and kernicterus (CASUALTY); the SNr is a recognized seat of seizure gating in rodent models (rodent data), but no human disorder has the SNr as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.2.4.5 THE SUBSTANTIA NIGRA PARS COMPACTA (SNc)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The dopamine neurons of the substantia nigra pars compacta (SNc) are born in the floor plate of the embryonic ventral midbrain, an unusual case of the floor plate acting as a neurogenic zone, specified by sonic hedgehog signalling with the transcription factors LMX1A, FOXA2, NURR1, and PITX3.

In the human they are generated in the late first and early second trimester, and their axons reach and begin innervating the striatum prenatally; the nigrostriatal system then matures across childhood, and striatal dopamine markers continue changing into adolescence.

The neurons acquire their adult burden progressively: enormous unmyelinated axonal arbors (single human SNc axons are estimated to support hundreds of thousands to millions of striatal synapses), autonomous pacemaking through calcium channels, and, from roughly the first decade onward in humans, accumulating neuromelanin.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Parkinson disease is the SNc's disease: alpha-synuclein pathology and neuronal death concentrate in its ventrolateral tier, and motor signs appear only after a large fraction of putaminal dopamine terminals is lost, classically estimated at more than half; the SNc is the prime CASUALTY, and the loss of its output is the CAUSE of the motor syndrome.

Its selective vulnerability profile is the field's central puzzle and is usually credited to the combination of huge axonal arbors, calcium-driven pacemaking, dopamine's own oxidative chemistry, and mitochondrial burden; MPTP poisoning and mitochondrial toxins reproduce the selectivity.

Neuromelanin-sensitive imaging and dopamine transporter scans of nigrostriatal integrity are diagnostic MARKERS; the SNc also degenerates in progressive supranuclear palsy and multiple system atrophy as a CASUALTY within wider pathology.

ENTRY: 2.2.5 THE NUCLEUS ACCUMBENS (SHELL AND CORE) AND THE VENTRAL PALLIDUM

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The nucleus accumbens is ventral striatum, born in the lateral ganglionic eminence with the rest of the striatal complex prenatally; the ventral pallidum is ventral pallidum proper, a medial ganglionic eminence derivative, so the pair recapitulates the dorsal striatopallidal origins one level down.

The shell and core distinction is molecular and hodological from early development, the shell grading into extended amygdala territory.

Dopamine innervation from the ventral tegmental area arrives prenatally, and the mesolimbic system then shows a marked adolescent chapter: human and animal work agree that reward-related accumbens reactivity peaks in adolescence, and rodent data show adolescent remodelling of accumbens dopamine and spine density (rodent data).

This adolescent window functions as a sensitive period for reward learning and for addiction risk.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Addiction is the accumbens's disorder: every major addictive drug raises dopamine in the accumbens, the shell first in initial reinforcement, with the transition to compulsion migrating dorsally; the accumbens is a CAUSE-level node, the site where the pathology of reinforcement is computed rather than a structure that degenerates.

In anhedonia and major depression, blunted accumbens reward response is one of the most reproducible functional MARKERS, and the accumbens is a deep brain stimulation target in treatment-resistant depression and obsessive-compulsive disorder.

The ventral pallidum, the accumbens's obligatory output and the site of hedonic hotspots in rodent work (rodent data), is a CAUSE-level candidate in anhedonia; no degenerative disease has either structure as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.2.6 THE CLAUSTRUM

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The claustrum derives from the lateral pallium, the same pallial territory that generates the insular cortex above it, and its principal neurons are born prenatally; the transcription factor NR4A2 (NURR1) marks claustral projection neurons across mammals.

Whether the claustrum is best described as a detached deep layer of the insula or a distinct pallial nucleus was argued for a century; modern genoarchitecture treats it as its own lateral pallial derivative, split from the insula by the extreme capsule.

Its extraordinary reciprocal connectivity with nearly the entire cortex must be assembled prenatally and postnatally, but no human timetable for claustral circuit maturation has been established, and no sensitive period is known; the structure's thin geometry has simply defied developmental imaging.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the claustrum as its primary seat, and that absence is itself informative for a structure connected to everything.

The claustrum shows a curious specific vulnerability as a MARKER: bilateral claustral signal change on imaging is a recognized feature of a rare febrile-infection-related epilepsy syndrome and of some autoimmune encephalitides, and claustral lesions have been reported with delusional syndromes in Parkinson disease.

A widely reported single-case observation that electrical stimulation near the left claustrum (in the white matter adjacent) reversibly interrupted consciousness fuelled the Crick and Koch hypothesis of the claustrum as a consciousness coordinator; it remains a single case, and the claustrum's clinical story is otherwise one of CASUALTY by adjacency in stroke and encephalitis.

ENTRY: 2.2.7 THE BASAL FOREBRAIN CHOLINERGIC COMPLEX (NUCLEUS BASALIS OF MEYNERT, MEDIAL SEPTAL NUCLEUS, DIAGONAL BAND OF BROCA)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The basal forebrain cholinergic neurons arise from the subpallium, principally the medial ganglionic eminence and preoptic territory under NKX2-1 and LHX8 control, the same lineage decision that separates them from pallidal GABAergic neighbours; nerve growth factor (NGF) signalling through the TrkA receptor is their lifelong survival and maintenance system.

They are generated prenatally in the human, and their ascending cholinergic innervation of cortex and hippocampus matures across infancy and childhood.

Their developmental importance is outsized: cholinergic tone gates cortical plasticity, and rodent work shows basal forebrain lesions block ocular dominance and auditory map plasticity during critical periods (rodent data), making this complex a regulator of other regions' sensitive periods rather than the host of one.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The nucleus basalis of Meynert degenerates early and severely in Alzheimer disease, the finding that launched the cholinergic hypothesis and the cholinesterase inhibitor drugs; the neurons are a CASUALTY of tau pathology (among the earliest sites of pretangle tau), their loss contributes to the cognitive syndrome as a downstream CAUSE, and basal forebrain volume on imaging is an early MARKER that can precede entorhinal atrophy.

The same complex degenerates in Parkinson disease dementia and dementia with Lewy bodies, where cholinergic loss is even deeper than in Alzheimer disease and tracks the hallucinations and cognitive fluctuation (CASUALTY).

Its selective vulnerability profile is classic: very large, sparsely redundant projection neurons with immense axonal arbors, lifelong NGF dependence, and early tau accumulation.

The medial septal division's loss disconnects hippocampal theta, an amnesia mechanism confirmed by rodent lesion work (rodent data).

ENTRY: 2.2.8 THE OLFACTORY BULB

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The olfactory bulb evaginates from the rostral telencephalon, its formation induced by the arrival of olfactory sensory axons from the nasal placode; in the human the bulbs are forming and receiving axons by the end of the first trimester, and failure of this induction (as in Kallmann syndrome) leaves the bulb absent.

Its projection neurons, the mitral and tufted cells, are born prenatally, but its interneurons, the granule and periglomerular cells, are supplied by a conveyor: the rostral migratory stream from the subventricular zone, which in rodents delivers new interneurons throughout life (rodent data).

In the human the rostral migratory stream is robust in infancy and then collapses; adult human olfactory bulb neurogenesis is essentially negligible by carbon-14 birth dating, a clear species difference from rodents.

The olfactory map of glomeruli, one glomerulus per receptor type, self-assembles by axon sorting prenatally and postnatally.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The olfactory bulb is the brain's early warning post: it is among the very first sites of alpha-synuclein pathology in Parkinson disease, and smell loss precedes motor signs by years, making the bulb an early CASUALTY and olfactory testing a practical MARKER; the Braak hypothesis even nominates it as one of two entry routes for the pathology, which would make it a gateway CAUSE, though this remains hypothesis.

Olfactory deficit is likewise an early MARKER in Alzheimer disease.

In Kallmann syndrome, failed olfactory axon and gonadotropin-releasing hormone neuron migration produces anosmia with hypogonadism; the bulb's absence is a CASUALTY of a migration failure and a diagnostic MARKER on imaging.

In coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) anosmia, the primary lesion is in the olfactory epithelium's support cells with secondary bulb involvement (CASUALTY).

ENTRY: 2.2.9 THE PIRIFORM CORTEX (PRIMARY OLFACTORY CORTEX)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The piriform cortex is three-layered paleocortex of the ventrolateral pallium, generated prenatally, and it is among the evolutionarily oldest cortices; its afferent wiring is distinctive from the start, with olfactory bulb axons arriving in the lateral olfactory tract and synapsing distally on layer I dendrites in a distributed, non-topographic pattern.

Odour representations here are learned ensembles rather than a spatial map, so the piriform's assembly is completed by experience: rodent work shows early postnatal odour learning (including the maternal odour imprinting window in the first postnatal week) engages piriform plasticity (rodent data).

No firm human maturation timetable for piriform circuitry has been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The piriform cortex is the most epileptogenic cortex in the mammalian brain: in rodents the deep piriform and adjacent endopiriform region, the area tempestas, is the site from which seizures are most easily kindled (rodent data), and human imaging implicates the piriform in focal epilepsy spread, making it a CAUSE-level amplifier in temporal lobe epilepsy.

Olfactory auras, the burnt-rubber smell preceding some temporal lobe seizures, are its clinical signature and a localizing MARKER.

The piriform and adjacent olfactory cortices accumulate early pathology in Alzheimer disease and Parkinson disease, consistent with the early smell loss of both (CASUALTY); no disorder has the piriform as its named primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.2.10 THE OLFACTORY TUBERCLE (OT)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The olfactory tubercle (OT) is ventral striatum in disguise: despite its cortical-looking surface it is a striatal, subpallial structure generated from the ventral lateral ganglionic eminence, complete with medium spiny neurons and dense dopamine innervation, assembled prenatally.

Its distinctive cap regions, the islands of Calleja, are treated in their own entry.

Its maturation timetable in the human has not been specifically characterized; what is known of its assembly comes from rodent birthdating within the ventral striatal complex (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No human disorder has the olfactory tubercle as its primary seat.

As ventral striatum receiving both olfactory bulb input and midbrain dopamine, it is implicated in rodent studies of drug reward and odour-motivation coupling (rodent data), and it presumably shares the ventral striatum's CAUSE-level role in addiction, but direct human evidence is thin because the structure is small and rarely resolved in imaging.

It receives early alpha-synuclein pathology in Parkinson disease along with the other olfactory structures (CASUALTY).

ENTRY: 2.2.11 THE ANTERIOR OLFACTORY NUCLEUS (AON)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The anterior olfactory nucleus (AON), better called anterior olfactory cortex, is a two-to-three layered pallial structure in the olfactory peduncle, generated prenatally from the ventral pallium with the rest of the olfactory cortices.

It is the hub of interhemispheric olfaction, projecting through the anterior commissure to the contralateral bulb and AON; this crossed wiring is assembled prenatally as the anterior commissure forms.

No dedicated human maturation timetable or sensitive period for the AON is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The AON has one striking distinction: it is consistently reported as the olfactory structure with the heaviest and earliest alpha-synuclein burden in Parkinson disease, and it also accumulates early tau in Alzheimer disease; in both it is a CASUALTY whose involvement helps explain early anosmia, and olfactory pathology at autopsy serves as a MARKER of Lewy body disease.
