No disorder has the AON as its primary seat, and its small size keeps it below clinical imaging resolution.

ENTRY: 2.2.12 THE ENDOPIRIFORM NUCLEUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The endopiriform nucleus is a sheet of glutamatergic neurons deep to the piriform cortex, developmentally allied to the claustrum: both are lateral or ventral pallial derivatives, and many schemes treat endopiriform and claustrum as one claustro-endopiriform complex, generated prenatally.

Its developmental timetable has not been separately characterized in the human; the claim that can be made honestly is pallial origin and prenatal genesis, established mainly through rodent and comparative genoarchitecture (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The endopiriform's one clinical claim is epileptological: the rodent area tempestas, the most seizure-prone site in the brain, lies in or immediately adjacent to the deep piriform and endopiriform region, and endopiriform neurons burst readily and synchronize wide territories (rodent data), a CAUSE-level property for limbic seizure generation.

Whether the human endopiriform plays the same igniter role in temporal lobe epilepsy is plausible but unproven.

No human disorder has the endopiriform nucleus as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.2.13 THE TAENIA TECTA

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The taenia tecta is a thin strip of medial olfactory cortex on the medial wall of the hemisphere, dorsal and ventral divisions, generated prenatally at the boundary where olfactory paleocortex meets medial pallial territory.

It is among the least studied structures in this catalogue: its cell birthdates and wiring have been described almost entirely in rodents (rodent data), and no human developmental timetable can be stated honestly.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the taenia tecta as its primary seat, and no human clinical syndrome is attached to it.

Rodent work has recently implicated the dorsal taenia tecta in stress responses and social odour processing (rodent data), which may eventually give the structure a clinical story, but at present its disease file is honestly empty.

ENTRY: 2.2.14 THE ISLANDS OF CALLEJA

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The islands of Calleja are dense clusters of tiny granule cells embedded in the olfactory tubercle, with a large medial island (the insula magna) tucked against the septum; the granule cells are subpallial, generated from the ventral lateral ganglionic eminence lineage that supplies olfactory bulb and tubercle granule cells.

In rodents these granule cells are born late, into the early postnatal period, and are among the smallest neurons in the brain, densely dopamine-receptive (rodent data); a human timetable has not been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the islands of Calleja as its primary seat, and no human clinical syndrome maps to them.

Their dense dopamine D3 receptor expression made them a pharmacological landmark in antipsychotic research, a MARKER-level relevance at most; recent rodent work links them to grooming behaviour, inviting speculation about compulsive disorders that remains speculation (rodent data).

ENTRY: 2.2.15 THE LATERAL SEPTAL NUCLEUS (LS)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The lateral septal nucleus (LS) is a GABAergic subpallial structure of the septal region, generated prenatally from septal neuroepithelium of the medial telencephalic wall; its dense hippocampal input via the fornix and its outputs to hypothalamus wire it as the hippocampus's gateway to motivated behaviour.

Rodent data dominate its developmental story: LS neurons are born in mid-gestation in the rat, and the LS is a rich site of vasopressin and oxytocin receptor expression whose densities are shaped by early social experience, one of the better candidates for an early-life sensitive period in social circuitry (rodent data).

No human maturation timetable for the LS has been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The LS's classic claim is septal rage: lesions of the septal region in rodents produce hyperdefensiveness (rodent data), and stimulation studies gave the septum an early reputation as a pleasure centre in both rodents and humans, evidence of CAUSE-level control over affect that never resolved into a named human disorder.

No human disorder has the LS as its primary seat.

Rodent models implicate the LS in anxiety regulation, social behaviour, and stress hormone gating via its hypothalamic projections, making it a candidate node in anxiety and social disorders (rodent data); human evidence remains indirect.

ENTRY: 2.3 THE DIENCEPHALON

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The diencephalon forms from the caudal part of the prosencephalon after the forebrain vesicle divides, visible in the human embryo by around week 5 of gestation.

In the modern prosomeric model it comprises three segments: prosomere 1 (pretectum), prosomere 2 (thalamus and epithalamus, including habenula and pineal), and prosomere 3 (prethalamus, source of the thalamic reticular nucleus and zona incerta); in this scheme the hypothalamus is reclassified as part of the secondary prosencephalon rather than the diencephalon proper, though this catalogue keeps the classical grouping.

A transient signalling centre, the zona limitans intrathalamica, forms between prethalamus and thalamus and secretes sonic hedgehog, patterning thalamic nuclei; it is the diencephalon's own organizer.

Thalamic neurogenesis is concentrated in the first half of gestation in the human, thalamocortical axons wait in the subplate before innervating cortex in the late second and third trimesters, and diencephalic circuits myelinate early relative to cortex.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Diencephalic disease is disease of relays and drives: thalamic strokes producing sensory loss, amnesia, or coma; Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome striking mammillary bodies and medial thalamus; hypothalamic disorders of feeding, sleep, and hormones; each is treated under its component's entry.

The paramedian diencephalon is selectively vulnerable to thiamine deficiency, the basis of Wernicke encephalopathy, where the region is a CASUALTY of a metabolic failure.

Diencephalic tumours in childhood produce the diencephalic syndrome of emaciation despite normal intake, a reminder that this division houses the body's set points.

ENTRY: 2.3.1 THE THALAMUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The thalamus proper arises from prosomere 2, its neurons generated in the first half of human gestation, patterned by sonic hedgehog from the zona limitans intrathalamica into rostral (GABAergic-fated) and caudal (glutamatergic, cortex-projecting) progenitor domains.

Assembly is a two-way handshake: thalamocortical axons navigate through the prethalamus and internal capsule, pause in the cortical subplate for weeks in the human, and invade layer IV in the late second and third trimesters, while corticothalamic axons reciprocate; the subplate waiting period is a known window of vulnerability in preterm infants.

Before sensory experience, spontaneous activity (retinal waves relayed through thalamus, established in rodents and ferrets; rodent data) refines thalamic maps.

Sensory thalamic nuclei myelinate and mature early; association nuclei tracking prefrontal cortex mature latest, into adolescence.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Thalamic strokes produce the sharpest lesion syndromes in neurology: ventral posterior stroke gives hemisensory loss and sometimes the Dejerine-Roussy central pain syndrome, paramedian bilateral stroke gives coma or profound amnesia and apathy; per stroke the thalamus is the CASUALTY.

Fatal familial insomnia, a genetic prion disease, selectively destroys the anterior and mediodorsal thalamus, abolishing sleep; it is the clearest disease whose primary seat is thalamic (CASUALTY of a genetic CAUSE).

The thalamus is a CASUALTY and MARKER in preterm brain injury, its volume tracking outcome; thalamocortical dysrhythmia is a proposed CAUSE-level mechanism in central pain, tinnitus, and some psychiatric states, still a hypothesis; and the thalamus, notably its pulvinar and mediodorsal divisions, shows consistent volume reduction in schizophrenia (MARKER, mechanism unsettled).

ENTRY: 2.3.1.1 LATERAL GENICULATE NUCLEUS (LGN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN) is generated in the caudal thalamic progenitor domain in the first half of human gestation, and its hallmark six-layered lamination, segregating eye of origin and magnocellular from parvocellular streams, emerges prenatally, with human LGN layers visible by around mid-gestation.

Eye-specific segregation is sculpted before vision by retinal waves, spontaneous correlated retinal activity, a mechanism established in ferret and rodent work (rodent and ferret data) and presumed conserved.

The LGN is the gateway to the best-defined critical period in neuroscience: monocular deprivation during early life shifts cortical ocular dominance, and while the plasticity is mainly cortical, the LGN relays and its cell sizes register the competition; in humans the sensitive period for amblyopia runs roughly from birth through age 7 to 8.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: In amblyopia, LGN cells serving the deprived or strabismic eye shrink, a CASUALTY and post-mortem MARKER of the competitive failure, though the disorder's seat is thalamocortical.

In glaucoma, degeneration extends beyond the retina into the LGN, transsynaptic CASUALTY, documented in primate and human studies.

LGN infarcts produce distinctive sectoranopias; in Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease variants and in some hereditary conditions the LGN participates but is never the primary seat; no disorder has the LGN as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.2 MEDIAL GENICULATE NUCLEUS (MGN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The medial geniculate nucleus (MGN) arises with the other caudal thalamic nuclei in the first half of human gestation, and its ventral, dorsal, and medial divisions differentiate prenatally, with tonotopic order inherited from the inferior colliculus input.

The auditory system is functional before birth: the human cochlea works from about gestational week 25 to 27, so the MGN relays sound in the third trimester, and foetal learning of the maternal voice shows the pathway is not just wired but recording.

Auditory cortical critical periods, defined mostly in rodent work (rodent data), depend on patterned MGN input; congenital deafness leaves the MGN small and its cortical target reorganized, and cochlear implantation before roughly age 3 to 4 exploits the human sensitive period.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No common disorder has the MGN as its primary seat.

It is a CASUALTY in rare thalamic strokes producing central hearing impairment, and post-mortem and imaging studies have reported MGN abnormalities in developmental dyslexia (smaller, disordered magnocellular-analogue neurons in one classic post-mortem series) and reduced MGN function in auditory processing accounts of dyslexia; this remains a MARKER-level finding with a contested mechanism.

The MGN is also a node in tinnitus models of thalamocortical dysrhythmia, a hypothesized CAUSE-level role that is not established.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.3 VENTRAL POSTEROLATERAL (VPL) AND VENTRAL POSTEROMEDIAL (VPM) NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The ventral posterolateral (VPL) and ventral posteromedial (VPM) nuclei, the somatosensory relay, are generated with the ventral tier prenatally, receiving medial lemniscal and trigeminal afferents whose somatotopy is laid down before birth.

The most famous assembly experiment lives here in rodents: the VPM contains barreloids, one cell cluster per whisker, and cutting the infraorbital nerve in the first postnatal days (rodent data) abolishes them, a critical period for map formation closing within roughly the first postnatal week in the rat.

Human somatosensory evoked responses are present preterm and mature rapidly over infancy; the human equivalent of the barreloid critical period has no established boundary.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: VPL and VPM are the seat of the classic thalamic sensory syndrome: infarct here produces contralateral hemianaesthesia, and in a fraction of patients the delayed, intractable Dejerine-Roussy central post-stroke pain; the nuclei are the CASUALTY, and the pain is read as deafferentation gone generative, a downstream CAUSE within the damaged circuit.

They are targets, not seats, in chronic pain neurosurgery and deep brain stimulation.

No degenerative disorder has VPL or VPM as its primary seat, though they participate in fatal familial insomnia and prion disease as CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.4 VENTRAL ANTERIOR (VA) AND VENTRAL LATERAL (VL) NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The ventral anterior (VA) and ventral lateral (VL) nuclei, the motor thalamus, are generated prenatally with the ventral tier, and their defining afferents partition them early: pallidal and nigral GABAergic input to VA and anterior VL, cerebellar (dentate nucleus) excitatory input to posterior VL, two territories that remain largely non-overlapping.

Their maturation tracks the motor system's protracted human timetable, with corticospinal and cerebellothalamic myelination continuing through infancy and childhood; no discrete sensitive period is established for the motor thalamus itself.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The motor thalamus is where the basal ganglia's and cerebellum's diseases become treatable: the cerebellar-recipient VL territory (the ventral intermediate nucleus of surgical nomenclature) is the premier deep brain stimulation and focused ultrasound target for essential tremor, and lesions here abolish tremor.

In Parkinson disease and dystonia the pallidal-recipient territory transmits pathological output, a conduit CAUSE within the circuit rather than a primary seat.

VA and VL are CASUALTY in thalamic stroke, where damage can produce hemiataxia or, paradoxically, abolish a pre-existing tremor; no disorder has VA or VL as its primary degenerative seat.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.5 MEDIODORSAL NUCLEUS (MD)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The mediodorsal nucleus (MD), the prefrontal thalamus, is generated prenatally, but its functional maturation is the slowest in the thalamus because it is yoked to the prefrontal cortex: MD-prefrontal connectivity continues refining through adolescence in humans, and rodent work shows adolescent MD activity is required for adult prefrontal function, a genuine sensitive period for cognitive circuitry (rodent data).

Developmental disruption of MD input in mouse models impairs prefrontal interneuron maturation and adult cognition (rodent data), making the MD one of the few thalamic nuclei with direct evidence of a developmental gating role.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The MD is central to Korsakoff syndrome: thiamine deficiency damages the MD, anterior nuclei, and mammillary bodies, and MD involvement is repeatedly implicated in the amnesia and confabulation; the MD is a CASUALTY of the metabolic insult.

Paramedian thalamic strokes that include the MD produce amnesia and profound apathy (CASUALTY).

In schizophrenia, reduced MD volume and MD-prefrontal functional connectivity are among the most replicated subcortical findings, currently a MARKER with an actively investigated CAUSE-level hypothesis, given the rodent evidence that MD hypofunction alone can reproduce prefrontal cognitive deficits (rodent data).

The MD is also destroyed early in fatal familial insomnia (CASUALTY).

ENTRY: 2.3.1.6 ANTERIOR THALAMIC NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The anterior thalamic nuclei (anteroventral, anteromedial, anterodorsal) are generated prenatally with the dorsal tier, wired from the start into the Papez circuit: mammillary body input via the mammillothalamic tract, dense reciprocity with cingulate and retrosplenial cortex, and hippocampal input via the fornix.

The anterodorsal nucleus houses the brain's most reliable head direction signal; in rat pups it is the earliest spatial signal to mature, adult-like around eye opening in the second postnatal week (rodent data).

No dedicated human maturation timetable for these nuclei has been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The anterior nuclei are memory-critical: strokes and Wernicke-Korsakoff pathology involving them or their mammillothalamic input produce diencephalic amnesia, and anterior thalamic damage is a CASUALTY-level lesion in alcohol-related amnesia alongside the mammillary bodies.

They are among the earliest and most severely affected sites in fatal familial insomnia (CASUALTY).

Their one therapeutic headline is epilepsy: deep brain stimulation of the anterior nucleus is an approved therapy for refractory focal epilepsy, exploiting the nucleus as a network chokepoint; here the nucleus is a lever, and its role in seizure propagation gives it a partial CAUSE-level status in the circuit.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.7 PULVINAR AND LATERAL POSTERIOR NUCLEUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The pulvinar, the largest human thalamic nucleus, is the thalamus's late bloomer: it expands enormously in primates, and in the human foetus it receives a documented late contribution of neurons migrating from the ganglionic eminence of the telencephalon into the diencephalon during the second trimester, a primate-specific migration described by Rakic and Sidman, alongside its diencephalic origin; the finding has been debated but remains the standard account, and it would make the pulvinar the one thalamic nucleus partly built from telencephalic parts.

Its maturation is correspondingly slow, tracking the association cortices it interconnects into childhood and adolescence.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the pulvinar as its primary seat, but it contributes two famous MARKERS: the pulvinar sign in variant Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (bilateral pulvinar signal on magnetic resonance imaging, diagnostic) and the pulvinar sign of Fabry disease (T1 hyperintensity).

Pulvinar damage in thalamic stroke contributes to neglect-like attentional deficits, consistent with its role in the attention network (CASUALTY).

In developmental conditions the pulvinar is implicated in the residual vision of blindsight after early visual cortex loss, where its colliculo-pulvinar pathway carries what survives, a compensator rather than a patient.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.8 INTRALAMINAR NUCLEI (CENTROMEDIAN AND PARAFASCICULAR)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The intralaminar nuclei, with the centromedian and parafascicular pair as their caudal core, are generated prenatally within the internal medullary lamina; the centromedian is greatly expanded in primates.

Their defining outputs to striatum and diffuse cortical targets, and their brainstem arousal inputs, are assembled prenatally, but no dedicated human maturation timetable or sensitive period has been established for them.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The intralaminar nuclei are arousal hardware: bilateral paramedian thalamic damage that includes them produces impaired consciousness, and central thalamic stimulation improved arousal in a landmark minimally conscious state case; in disorders of consciousness they are a CASUALTY whose loss is close to a proximate CAUSE of the unconsciousness.

The centromedian is an established deep brain stimulation target in Lennox-Gastaut syndrome and generalized epilepsy, and the centromedian-parafascicular complex degenerates measurably in Parkinson disease, an underpublicized CASUALTY that may contribute to attentional symptoms.

No disorder has the intralaminar nuclei as its named primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.9 THE THALAMIC RETICULAR NUCLEUS (TRN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The thalamic reticular nucleus (TRN) is the odd one out in the thalamus: it is GABAergic and arises not from the thalamus proper but from the prethalamus (prosomere 3), forming the inhibitory shell through which all thalamocortical and corticothalamic axons must pass; its neurons are generated prenatally.

Its signature rhythm, the sleep spindle, matures on a known human timetable: spindles emerge in the electroencephalogram at around 6 to 8 weeks after term birth, one of the landmark maturational events of infant sleep.

Rodent work shows TRN sectors are wired modality by modality as thalamocortical axons grow through it (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The TRN is a CAUSE-level structure in absence epilepsy: the thalamocortical spike-wave oscillation of absence seizures depends on TRN burst firing through T-type calcium channels, the mechanistic target of ethosuximide, established in rodent and genetic models (rodent data) and consistent with human genetics of T-type channel variants.

Reduced sleep spindle density is one of the most replicated sleep MARKERS in schizophrenia, and TRN dysfunction is a leading hypothesis for it, a candidate CAUSE for the sensory gating deficits of the disorder.

No degenerative disease has the TRN as its primary seat; it is a CASUALTY within broader thalamic pathology such as fatal familial insomnia.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.10 THE NUCLEUS REUNIENS (RE) AND THE MIDLINE THALAMUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The nucleus reuniens (RE) and the other midline nuclei are generated prenatally along the third-ventricle wall of the thalamus, and the RE's defining position, as the only robust bidirectional bridge between hippocampus and medial prefrontal cortex, is a wiring fact established almost entirely in rodents (rodent data).

Rodent work places RE-dependent prefrontal-hippocampal coordination among the later-maturing memory functions, but no human developmental timetable for the RE exists and none can honestly be stated.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No human disorder has the nucleus reuniens as its primary seat.

Rodent models give it CAUSE-level roles in generalizing fear memories (a post-traumatic stress disorder mechanism candidate) and in coordinating slow oscillations during memory consolidation, and RE dysfunction is proposed in schizophrenia's prefrontal-hippocampal desynchrony (rodent data); human confirmation is currently beyond imaging resolution.

The midline nuclei are damaged in Wernicke encephalopathy's periventricular lesions as a CASUALTY.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.11 THE PARAVENTRICULAR THALAMUS (PVT)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The paraventricular thalamus (PVT) is the dorsal midline nucleus under the third ventricle roof, generated prenatally; it is unusual among thalamic nuclei in receiving dense hypothalamic and brainstem arousal input, including orexin, and projecting to accumbens, amygdala, and prefrontal cortex.

Rodent work distinguishes anterior and posterior PVT and shows its stress and salience functions come online across postnatal development, but timings are rodent-only (rodent data); no human timetable exists.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No human disorder has the PVT as its primary seat.

In rodent models it is a rising CAUSE-level candidate in addiction, specifically opiate withdrawal aversion and cue-driven relapse, and in stress and arousal disorders, through its orexin input and accumbens output (rodent data).

Human evidence is limited to imaging-level midline thalamic findings; the honest statement is that the PVT is a structure whose clinical chapter has not yet been written.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.12 THE LATERAL DORSAL NUCLEUS (LD)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The lateral dorsal nucleus (LD) is generated prenatally in the dorsal tier and functions as a limbic associate of the anterior nuclei, connected with retrosplenial and cingulate cortex and hippocampal formation, and carrying head direction signals in the rodent (rodent data).

It is sparsely studied; no human maturation timetable or sensitive period can be stated for the LD.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the lateral dorsal nucleus as its primary seat.

It is presumed to share the Papez circuit's CASUALTY status in diencephalic amnesia when strokes or Wernicke pathology extend into it, and rodent lesion work implicates it in spatial learning (rodent data), but a distinct human LD syndrome has never been described.

ENTRY: 2.3.1.13 THE POSTERIOR VENTRAL MEDIAL NUCLEUS (VMpo)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The posterior ventral medial nucleus (VMpo) is a proposed primate elaboration: Craig and colleagues described it in the 1990s as a parvalbumin-rich posterior thalamic zone, greatly expanded in humans, serving as the dedicated relay from spinal lamina I to interoceptive insular cortex.

Its very existence as a distinct nucleus is contested: critics led by Willis and colleagues argue the territory is part of the classical posterior nuclear group and that lamina I input to the ventrobasal complex was underweighted; this catalogue records the dispute rather than resolving it.

Accordingly, no developmental account specific to VMpo exists; nothing honest can be said of its timetable beyond general posterior thalamic prenatal genesis.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has VMpo as its established primary seat, and clinical claims inherit the anatomical dispute.

Craig's framework attributes central post-stroke pain of the Dejerine-Roussy type partly to lesions disrupting VMpo's lamina I relay, which would make VMpo a CASUALTY whose loss is a CAUSE of the pain; the rival account assigns the same lesions to the classical ventral posterior and posterior nuclei.

Resolution would require lesion mapping at a precision human imaging has not yet delivered.

ENTRY: 2.3.2 THE HYPOTHALAMUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The hypothalamus forms from the ventral forebrain very early, and the modern prosomeric model reassigns it from the diencephalon to the rostral secondary prosencephalon, continuous with the telencephalon above it; this catalogue keeps the classical placement while noting the reclassification.

It is patterned by sonic hedgehog from the underlying prechordal plate, and its peptidergic neuron types (oxytocin, vasopressin, orexin, agouti-related peptide and proopiomelanocortin lineages, and the rest) are specified by a dense combinatorial transcription factor code during the first half of gestation in the human.

It is functional before birth, running foetal endocrine axes, and several of its circuits keep assembling postnatally: rodent data show the arcuate feeding projections are wired by a leptin-dependent postnatal critical period, and perinatal gonadal hormones sexually differentiate several hypothalamic nuclei (rodent data), with human counterparts such as the sexually dimorphic intermediate nucleus maturing over years.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Hypothalamic disease is disease of set points: narcolepsy (orexin neuron loss), genetic and hypothalamic obesity, diabetes insipidus, precocious or failed puberty, and circadian disruption all seat here, each under its nucleus's entry.

Craniopharyngioma and its surgery produce hypothalamic obesity, a CASUALTY syndrome demonstrating that destruction of the weighing machinery, not gluttony, drives the weight.

Kallmann syndrome and congenital hypogonadotropic hypogonadism are developmental CAUSE disorders of gonadotropin-releasing hormone neuron migration into the hypothalamus.

Prader-Willi syndrome behaves as a genetic disorder whose insatiable hunger points at hypothalamic feeding circuitry as the proximate CAUSE, with oxytocin neuron deficits reported.

ENTRY: 2.3.2.1 PARAVENTRICULAR NUCLEUS (PVN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The paraventricular nucleus (PVN) differentiates in the first half of human gestation under the transcription factors SIM1, OTP, and BRN2; mutations in SIM1 halve PVN neuron numbers in mice (rodent data) and cause obesity in mice and humans, the clearest single-gene handle on the nucleus.

Its magnocellular oxytocin and vasopressin neurons and its parvocellular corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH), thyrotropin-releasing hormone, and autonomic populations are specified prenatally, and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis it commands is functional at birth.

Its stress circuitry hosts a well-documented early-life sensitive period: rodent maternal care programs CRH expression and glucocorticoid receptor feedback for life via epigenetic mechanisms (rodent data), with human early adversity showing parallel signatures.

The arcuate-to-PVN feeding projection is completed postnatally in a leptin-gated window (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: SIM1 haploinsufficiency and melanocortin-4 receptor pathway failure produce severe obesity through PVN dysfunction, a genetic CAUSE seated here.

Chronic stress disorders and melancholic depression show CRH overdrive originating in the PVN, a CAUSE-level mechanism, with elevated CRH neuron numbers in post-mortem depression studies serving as a MARKER.

PVN vasopressin failure after pituitary or hypothalamic damage causes central diabetes insipidus (CASUALTY); in Prader-Willi syndrome reduced PVN oxytocin neurons are a reported MARKER and candidate mechanism.

ENTRY: 2.3.2.2 SUPRAOPTIC NUCLEUS (SON)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The supraoptic nucleus (SON) shares the PVN's magnocellular lineage, specified prenatally under OTP and SIM1 control, its oxytocin and vasopressin neurons differentiating in the first half of human gestation and sending axons down the pituitary stalk to the posterior pituitary before birth.

The system is functional at birth, running neonatal water balance and participating in the oxytocin surges of birth itself.

Its celebrated plasticity is adult and reversible: in lactation and dehydration the SON remodels its glial coverage and synapses within days, the textbook case of structural plasticity on demand (established in rodents; rodent data); no early sensitive period is described for the SON.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Destruction of the SON and its tract, by pituitary surgery, trauma, tumours, or autoimmune and infiltrative disease, causes central diabetes insipidus, the SON as CASUALTY; notably, damage below the median eminence often spares enough function because the axons are lost but the cell bodies survive.

The SON is the effector, not the seat, in the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion, where release is driven inappropriately (mechanistically a CAUSE only when the drive is hypothalamic).

Familial neurohypophyseal diabetes insipidus, from mutant vasopressin precursors that poison the magnocellular neurons, is a genetic CAUSE whose degeneration seats in the SON and PVN.

No other disorder has the SON as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.3.2.3 ARCUATE NUCLEUS (ARC)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The arcuate nucleus (ARC) differentiates prenatally at the base of the third ventricle beside the median eminence, its two antagonist feeding populations, agouti-related peptide (AgRP) neurons and proopiomelanocortin (POMC) neurons, specified in the first half of gestation, alongside its growth-hormone-releasing, dopamine, and kisspeptin populations.

Its assembly has the best-documented hypothalamic critical period: in the mouse, ARC projections to the PVN and elsewhere are established postnatally under the control of a neonatal leptin surge, and early over- or undernutrition permanently rewires the feeding circuit (rodent data), a leading mechanism for developmental programming of obesity; the human equivalent likely falls in late gestation, when the human leptin surge occurs, but its boundaries are not established.

Kisspeptin neurons of the ARC awaken the reproductive axis at puberty.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The ARC is the seat of monogenic obesity: leptin, leptin receptor, POMC, and melanocortin-4 receptor pathway mutations disable its energy-balance computation, a genetic CAUSE, and setmelanotide treats the downstream pathway.

Its position outside the blood-brain barrier makes it selectively vulnerable: it is the structure damaged by monosodium glutamate in neonatal rodents (rodent data) and is exposed to circulating insults generally.

Hypothalamic inflammation and gliosis of the ARC in diet-induced obesity is a reported human imaging MARKER and rodent CAUSE-level mechanism (rodent data).

In anorexia of ageing and cachexia the ARC circuits are implicated as CAUSE-level mediators; kisspeptin system failure here produces hypogonadotropic hypogonadism.

ENTRY: 2.3.2.4 LATERAL HYPOTHALAMIC AREA (LHA)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The lateral hypothalamic area (LHA) is an old, loosely organized territory traversed by the medial forebrain bundle, its neurons generated prenatally, with rodent birthdating placing lateral hypothalamic neurons among the earliest-born in the hypothalamus (rodent data).

Its two signature peptide populations, the orexin (hypocretin) neurons and the melanin-concentrating hormone neurons, are intermingled but distinct lineages specified prenatally.

Orexin neurons number on the order of 50,000 to 80,000 in the adult human, a small population with brain-wide reach; sleep architecture consolidates across infancy as these arousal systems mature, but no precise human timetable for orexin system maturation is established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Narcolepsy type 1 is the LHA's disease: selective autoimmune-mediated loss of nearly all orexin neurons, strongly linked to human leukocyte antigen DQB1 06:02 and triggered in some cases by influenza infection or one adjuvanted vaccine, produces sleepiness and cataplexy; the orexin neurons are the CASUALTY, their absence the proximate CAUSE, and low cerebrospinal fluid orexin is the diagnostic MARKER.

The melanin-concentrating hormone neurons beside them survive, a selectivity pointing at an antigen-specific process.

Classical lateral hypothalamic lesions produce aphagia and adipsia (rodent lesion data; rodent data), and LHA damage from tumours reproduces it in humans (CASUALTY); the LHA is also a CAUSE-level node in addiction models through orexin's drive on the ventral tegmental area (rodent data).

ENTRY: 2.3.2.5 TUBEROMAMMILLARY NUCLEUS (TMN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The tuberomammillary nucleus (TMN) of the posterior hypothalamus is the brain's only source of neuronal histamine, a small population (tens of thousands of neurons in the human) specified prenatally in the posterior, mammillary-region hypothalamus.

Rodent data show histaminergic neurons appear in late gestation and the histamine system matures postnatally (rodent data); no human maturation timetable has been established.

Its wake-active firing pattern, on in waking, silent in rapid eye movement sleep, is a fixed property of the adult system whose developmental onset is not characterized in humans.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the TMN as its established primary seat.

Its clearest clinical fingerprints are pharmacological: antihistamines that cross the blood-brain barrier cause sedation by silencing its output, and the wake-promoting drug pitolisant, a histamine H3 receptor inverse agonist licensed for narcolepsy, works by disinhibiting it.

In narcolepsy, an increased number of histaminergic neurons has been reported post mortem, an intriguing compensatory MARKER; TMN involvement is proposed in the sleepiness of Parkinson disease and in disorders of arousal generally, as a CASUALTY within wider degeneration, but evidence is limited.

ENTRY: 2.3.3 THE HABENULA (LATERAL AND MEDIAL)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The habenula is epithalamus, arising from the dorsal part of prosomere 2 beside the pineal, its medial and lateral divisions specified prenatally as distinct lineages; its output bundle, the fasciculus retroflexus, is one of the earliest and most stereotyped tracts of the embryonic brain.

In zebrafish and rodents the habenula is a model of brain asymmetry, with left-right differences under Nodal signalling control (fish and rodent data); whether the human habenula is functionally lateralized is not established.

The medial habenula's interpeduncular projection is the densest cholinergic-plus-substance-P pathway in the brain, assembled prenatally.

No human postnatal maturation timetable for the habenula has been established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The lateral habenula is depression's most specific circuit candidate: it encodes negative reward prediction errors, and its sustained hyperactivity, including nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide-dependent bursting shown in rodent models (rodent data), suppresses dopamine and serotonin systems; here it is a CAUSE-level node, and the rapid antidepressant ketamine blocks lateral habenula bursting in those models.

Human deep brain stimulation of the lateral habenula for treatment-resistant depression has been reported in small numbers of cases, evidence at case level only.

The medial habenula's nicotinic receptor circuitry governs nicotine aversion and withdrawal in rodents (rodent data), a CAUSE-level role in addiction's intake ceiling; degeneration of the fasciculus retroflexus after stimulant exposure in rodents (rodent data) is a proposed CASUALTY mechanism.

No degenerative disease has the habenula as its primary seat.

ENTRY: 2.3.3.1 THE HABENULA-MONOAMINE FEEDBACK HUB (PUC)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: This entry is a previously uncharacterized construct (PUC) of the corpus: the lateral habenula considered as the unified negative-feedback controller of both monoamine systems, so its development is the development of its parts.

The lateral habenula, its pallidal and prefrontal inputs via the stria medullaris, its output through the fasciculus retroflexus, and the rostromedial tegmental nucleus relay all assemble prenatally, the fasciculus retroflexus being among the first tracts in the embryonic brain.

When the assembled loop first computes a negative reward prediction error has not been measured in any species; developmental recordings of habenular responses to reward omission would settle it, and none exist.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The hub's disorder is depression, and specifically anhedonia and behavioural despair: chronic lateral habenula hyperactivity simultaneously throttles dopamine and serotonin, the corpus's point that one node can silence both monoamine systems at once; per depression this hub is a CAUSE-level candidate with the strongest circuit-level evidence of any single structure, though the evidence base is dominated by rodent models (rodent data).

Ketamine's block of habenular burst firing in rodent models (rodent data) is the leading mechanistic account of rapid antidepressant action through this hub.

Because the construct is functional rather than cytoarchitectonic, it has no separate lesion literature; its CASUALTY and MARKER files are those of the lateral habenula entry above.

ENTRY: 2.3.4 THE PINEAL GLAND

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The pineal gland evaginates from the roof of the diencephalon (the epithalamus of prosomere 2) in the second month of human gestation, and its pinealocytes are developmental cousins of photoreceptors, sharing lineage machinery; in non-mammalian vertebrates the same organ is directly photosensitive, and mammals rewired it to receive light information neurally through the circuitous sympathetic route the corpus describes.

Melatonin rhythms are not mature at birth: the human infant establishes a robust circadian melatonin rhythm at roughly 2 to 3 months of age, one reason newborn sleep ignores the clock, and before that the infant uses maternal melatonin transferred across the placenta and in milk.

Pineal calcification accumulates from childhood onward and is so common as to be a radiological landmark; its functional significance is debated.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No common disorder has the pineal as its primary seat; its clinical files are mostly tumours and timing.

Pineal region tumours (germinomas, pineoblastomas) compress the tectum to cause Parinaud syndrome and obstruct cerebrospinal fluid flow; pineoblastoma shares lineage with retinoblastoma, and bilateral retinoblastoma with pineoblastoma is called trilateral retinoblastoma, the photoreceptor kinship turned malignant (the pineal as seat of the tumour, a CAUSE in the oncological sense).

Loss of the melatonin rhythm after pinealectomy or in tetraplegia (which severs the sympathetic relay) disturbs sleep timing, and melatonin profiles serve as the standard MARKER of circadian phase in circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders; Smith-Magenis syndrome inverts melatonin timing, with the pineal as effector rather than seat.

ENTRY: 2.3.5 THE SUBTHALAMUS AND THE ZONA INCERTA THE ZONA INCERTA (ZI)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The zona incerta (ZI), the under-named grey sheet of the classical subthalamus, derives from the prethalamus (prosomere 3), the same GABAergic territory that produces the thalamic reticular nucleus; its neurons are generated prenatally and organize into rostral, dorsal, ventral, and caudal sectors with distinct chemistries.

Rodent work shows the ZI is a developmental crossroads through which thalamocortical axons navigate, and that its GABAergic projections to higher-order thalamus mature postnatally (rodent data).

No human maturation timetable for the ZI has been established, fitting a structure whose very name means zone of uncertainty.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: No disorder has the zona incerta as its primary seat.

Its clinical importance is as a target: the caudal ZI and the adjacent posterior subthalamic area are effective deep brain stimulation sites for tremor, including essential tremor and parkinsonian tremor, arguably rivalling the thalamic ventral intermediate nucleus, so the ZI enters medicine as a lever on circuits that malfunction elsewhere.

Rodent studies give it CAUSE-level candidacy in binge eating (ZI GABAergic stimulation evokes rapid feeding), in defensive behaviour, and in sleep regulation (rodent data); human confirmation is awaited, and incidental effects of ZI-region stimulation in patients, including reports of altered mood and appetite, are the current human evidence.

ENTRY: 2.4 THE BRAINSTEM

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The brainstem forms from the two most caudal primary brain vesicles, the mesencephalon (midbrain) and the rhombencephalon (hindbrain), the latter dividing into the metencephalon (pons and cerebellum) and the myelencephalon (medulla). Its defining developmental feature is segmentation: the hindbrain transiently divides into repeating segments called rhombomeres, each a lineage-restricted compartment with its own Hox-gene address, and the cranial nerve nuclei are laid down segment by segment along this rostrocaudal grid.

Dorsoventral patterning, driven by Sonic hedgehog (Shh) from the floor plate ventrally and bone morphogenetic proteins from the roof plate dorsally, sorts neurons into motor columns ventrally and sensory columns dorsally, so the adult arrangement of cranial nerve nuclei is a direct readout of this two-axis grid. The isthmic organizer at the midbrain-hindbrain boundary, a signalling centre secreting fibroblast growth factor eight and Wnt1, patterns the midbrain and the rostral hindbrain and is where the dopaminergic and serotonergic groups arise.

Timings above the level of gross vesicle formation are established mostly in mouse and chick; human rhombomere boundaries are transient and are marked in the fifth to sixth gestational week.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The brainstem as a whole is the site where a lesion of a few cubic millimetres can be fatal, because the respiratory and cardiovascular centres are packed there. It is a CASUALTY in stroke (the pons and medulla have distinctive syndromes), in central pontine myelinolysis, in brainstem gliomas of childhood (diffuse intrinsic pontine glioma), and in the late Braak stages of Parkinson and Alzheimer disease, whose earliest lesions are in fact brainstem nuclei. Its selective vulnerability profile is dominated by the long-axon, high-metabolic monoaminergic nuclei described below.

ENTRY: 2.4.1 THE MIDBRAIN

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The midbrain (mesencephalon) is the only primary vesicle that does not subdivide. Its identity is set by the isthmic organizer at its caudal edge and by the zona limitans and forebrain signals at its rostral edge. The tectum (superior and inferior colliculi) develops dorsally from the alar plate, and the tegmentum, including the dopaminergic groups and the red nucleus, develops ventrally from the basal plate under Shh.

The dopaminergic neurons of the midbrain floor plate are a signature product of this region; in mouse they are born between embryonic day ten and a half and day fourteen and a half under combined Shh and Wnt signalling (rodent data). Human midbrain dopaminergic neurogenesis occupies roughly the sixth to eleventh gestational week, but precise human birthdating is less firmly fixed than the mouse timeline.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The midbrain is a CASUALTY in Parkinson disease (substantia nigra), in progressive supranuclear palsy (which strikes midbrain tegmentum and produces the vertical gaze palsy and midbrain atrophy seen as the hummingbird sign), and in midbrain stroke syndromes (Weber, Benedikt, Claude). Its dopaminergic populations carry the selective vulnerability that makes this region the epicentre of Parkinsonism.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.1 SUPERIOR COLLICULUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The superior colliculus develops from the dorsal (alar) midbrain and is one of the oldest and most conserved visuomotor structures in the vertebrate brain, homologous to the optic tectum of non-mammals. Its layered retinotopic map is assembled by activity-independent molecular gradients, most famously the Eph receptor and ephrin gradients that guide retinal axons to their correct anteroposterior and mediolateral positions, followed by activity-dependent refinement of the map.

The registration of the visual, auditory, and somatosensory maps in the deep layers is refined postnatally and depends on early sensory experience; in rodent and ferret work, altering one modality shifts the alignment of the others (rodent and carnivore data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The superior colliculus has no common primary degenerative disease of its own, and this must be said plainly rather than papered over. It is a CASUALTY in dorsal midbrain (Parinaud) syndrome, usually from pineal region tumours or hydrocephalus compressing the tectum, producing upgaze palsy and light-near dissociation. Its survival when the primary visual cortex is destroyed is the anatomical basis of blindsight, so it is better known for what it preserves than for any disease that destroys it.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.2 INFERIOR COLLICULUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The inferior colliculus develops from the dorsal midbrain alar plate just caudal to the superior colliculus. It is the obligatory midbrain relay of the auditory pathway, and its tonotopic (frequency) map is set up before hearing onset by molecular cues and then sharpened by early auditory experience. Its central nucleus receives convergent input from every lower brainstem auditory nucleus, and this convergence is assembled progressively during the perinatal period.

Detailed maturation timing comes largely from rodent and cat studies keyed to the onset of hearing (rodent and carnivore data); the human tonotopic organisation is present by the time the cochlea becomes functional in the third trimester.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The inferior colliculus has no signature human neurodegenerative disease and this should be stated rather than invented. In genetically seizure-prone rodents it is the trigger zone for audiogenic (sound-induced) seizures, but that is a rodent model, not a human disorder. It is a CASUALTY in midbrain stroke and in disorders of central auditory processing, and it is highly metabolically active, which makes it one of the earliest sites of damage in thiamine deficiency (Wernicke encephalopathy) and in mitochondrial disease.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.3 PERIAQUEDUCTAL GRAY (PAG)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The periaqueductal gray develops from the midbrain around the cerebral aqueduct, at the interface of alar and basal plate derivatives, which is consistent with its mixed sensory-modulatory and autonomic-motor functions. Its columnar organisation, dorsomedial, dorsolateral, lateral, and ventrolateral columns each with distinct defensive and autonomic roles, is present early and is elaborated by descending forebrain input across postnatal maturation.

The descending pain-modulatory pathway from the PAG to the rostral ventromedial medulla and dorsal horn is functional early but its opioidergic tone matures with the pain system; developmental timing here is characterised mainly in rodent (rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The PAG is implicated as a CAUSE-adjacent hub in migraine, where imaging shows increased iron deposition and altered function in the PAG and it may act as a generator of attacks, though whether the change causes attacks or marks them is unsettled, so its role there is best classed as a MARKER with a possible causal component. It is a CAUSE hub in panic and defensive-behaviour dysregulation when its ventrolateral or dorsal columns are imbalanced, and a CASUALTY in midbrain lesions producing central pain. Its opioid-rich tissue is the target of morphine analgesia and a site of tolerance.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.3.1 THE PERIAQUEDUCTAL GRAY PAIN MODULATION HUB (PUC)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: Considered specifically as the organised hub of descending pain modulation, this construct assembles as the PAG-to-rostral-ventromedial-medulla-to-dorsal-horn axis matures. The anatomy is present at birth, but the balance of ON-cells and OFF-cells in the medulla and the opioidergic tone of the loop mature postnatally, which is part of why newborn pain processing differs from adult, with descending inhibition relatively immature early. The developmental characterisation of this loop is largely rodent (rodent data), and no honest human timescale for the maturation of descending analgesia can be stated with precision.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: This hub is a CASUALTY in the chronic pain states in which descending inhibition fails and descending facilitation dominates, a shift documented in fibromyalgia and other central sensitisation syndromes where the OFF-to-ON balance tilts toward facilitation. It is the site of action, and therefore of tolerance and dependence, for opioid analgesics, so it is causally central to opioid pharmacology. It is a MARKER in migraine by the iron and functional changes noted above.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.4 VENTRAL TEGMENTAL AREA (VTA)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The dopaminergic neurons of the VTA arise from the midbrain floor plate, a region that acts unusually as a neurogenic zone rather than a purely non-neurogenic organiser. In mouse, VTA dopaminergic neurogenesis peaks around embryonic day eleven and a half, slightly later than the substantia nigra population, and is driven by Shh and Wnt1 with the transcription factors Otx2, Lmx1a/b, Foxa2, Nurr1, and Pitx3 specifying and maintaining the dopaminergic phenotype (rodent data). Otx2 is a key determinant that biases progenitors toward the VTA rather than the nigral fate.

Human midbrain dopaminergic neurons are generated across roughly the sixth to eleventh gestational weeks; the mesolimbic and mesocortical projections then extend and are refined into adolescence, which matters because prefrontal dopamine innervation is still maturing in the teenage years.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The VTA is the CAUSE hub of addiction: every drug of abuse raises dopamine in the nucleus accumbens by acting on or through the VTA, and drug-induced plasticity here initiates the disorder. It is implicated in depression (reduced reward drive) and in schizophrenia (the mesolimbic dopamine hypothesis of positive symptoms), where its signalling is a CAUSE-level contributor. Notably, in Parkinson disease the VTA dopaminergic neurons are relatively spared compared with the substantia nigra, so its selective vulnerability is lower; the differential is attributed to calcium-handling, calbindin expression, and pacemaking differences between the two populations.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.5 RED NUCLEUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The red nucleus develops from the ventral midbrain (basal plate) tegmentum. In humans the magnocellular (rubrospinal) part is prominent prenatally and in infancy and then regresses, while the parvocellular part, connected to the inferior olive and cerebellum through the Guillain-Mollaret triangle, predominates in the adult. This ontogenetic shift mirrors the phylogenetic replacement of the rubrospinal tract by the corticospinal tract in humans.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The red nucleus is a CASUALTY in midbrain stroke and in lesions of the Guillain-Mollaret triangle, which produce Holmes tremor (rubral tremor), a slow, large-amplitude tremor with rest, postural, and intention components that appears weeks to months after the causative lesion. Its intact rubrospinal output distinguishes decorticate posturing (lesion above the red nucleus) from decerebrate posturing (lesion below it), a bedside localisation of catastrophic injury. It has no primary degenerative disease of its own.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.6 THE ROSTROMEDIAL TEGMENTAL NUCLEUS (RMTg)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The RMTg is a recently defined GABAergic nucleus of the caudal midbrain tegmentum, identified in the 2000s, and its developmental origin is not yet settled. Available work places it among tegmental GABAergic populations, but the lineage and birthdate of the RMTg cannot be stated honestly at present; what would settle it is genetic fate-mapping of the transcription factors that mark its neurons, work that has been done for neighbouring groups but not definitively for this one.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: As the master brake on dopamine, the RMTg is implicated in depression and in aversion-driven states, where its excessive inhibition of dopamine neurons, driven by the lateral habenula, can suppress reward and is a CAUSE-level contributor to anhedonia. It is a target in addiction because drugs that disinhibit dopamine act partly by silencing RMTg. Its disease role is defined functionally rather than by a named degeneration, and much of the evidence is rodent (rodent data); a specific human degenerative disorder of the RMTg is not established and this should be said rather than invented.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.7 THE INTERPEDUNCULAR NUCLEUS (IPN)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The interpeduncular nucleus is an unpaired midline nucleus at the ventral midbrain-hindbrain junction, the terminus of the medial habenula's private line through the fasciculus retroflexus. The medial-habenula-to-IPN pathway is a conserved, evolutionarily ancient circuit, and its assembly depends on the correct guidance of the fasciculus retroflexus; developmental studies are largely in rodent and zebrafish (rodent and fish data), and a precise human timeline is not established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The IPN is central to the aversive side of nicotine and addiction: the medial-habenula-to-IPN pathway carries the aversive signal that limits nicotine intake, and variation in the nicotinic receptor subunit genes (CHRNA5/A3/B4) expressed here is a CAUSE-level genetic risk factor for heavy smoking and dependence. It is implicated in anxiety and mood regulation. It has no named primary degenerative disease, and its disorder role is defined through this circuit rather than through cell loss.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.8 THE CUNEIFORM NUCLEUS AND THE MESENCEPHALIC LOCOMOTOR REGION

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The mesencephalic locomotor region, comprising the cuneiform nucleus and the adjacent pedunculopontine nucleus, develops in the dorsolateral midbrain-pontine tegmentum. Its glutamatergic cuneiform neurons drive high-speed defensive locomotion. The circuitry linking it to reticulospinal command neurons and spinal central pattern generators is worked out mainly in rodent, cat, and lamprey preparations (rodent, carnivore, and lower-vertebrate data), and a specific human developmental timeline for this region is not established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The mesencephalic locomotor region is implicated in Parkinsonian gait freezing and postural instability, where its output is disrupted downstream of basal ganglia dysfunction, making it partly a CASUALTY of the wider Parkinsonian state. The pedunculopontine part is an experimental deep brain stimulation target for gait freezing, with mixed results. There is no primary degenerative disease confined to the cuneiform nucleus.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.9 THE EDINGER-WESTPHAL NUCLEUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The Edinger-Westphal nucleus develops in the dorsal midbrain near the oculomotor complex and is now understood to contain two distinct populations: a classic preganglionic parasympathetic part that projects to the ciliary ganglion for pupil constriction and lens accommodation, and a centrally projecting peptidergic part that expresses urocortin and is involved in stress and consummatory behaviour. These two populations have different connections and probably different developmental programmes, but the lineage separation is not fully resolved (much of the work is rodent, rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The preganglionic part is a CASUALTY in dorsal midbrain lesions affecting the pupillary light reflex and in the light-near dissociation of Parinaud syndrome. The urocortin-expressing centrally projecting part is implicated in alcohol preference and stress responses in rodent models, where it is a CAUSE-level modulator of consumption (rodent data). Argyll Robertson pupils of neurosyphilis localise to the pretectum rather than to this nucleus, a distinction worth keeping. It has no named primary degeneration.

ENTRY: 2.4.1.10 THE CENTRAL MESENCEPHALIC RETICULAR FORMATION (cMRF)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The central mesencephalic reticular formation is a poorly delimited zone of the midbrain tegmentum that relays saccade commands to the pontine gaze generator. Because it is a reticular region rather than a discrete nucleus, its developmental origin has not been separately characterised, and no honest developmental timeline for the cMRF as a distinct entity can be stated. What would settle it is connectional and transcriptomic mapping distinguishing it from surrounding reticular formation.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The cMRF is a CASUALTY in midbrain lesions that disrupt horizontal saccades and gaze, and it participates in the gaze abnormalities of progressive supranuclear palsy and midbrain stroke. It has no primary disease of its own, and its clinical footprint is that of the wider midbrain oculomotor circuitry rather than a selective vulnerability. This is an intentionally short entry, matching the thinness of the evidence.

ENTRY: 2.4.2 THE PONS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The pons develops from the metencephalon, the rostral part of the rhombencephalon, sharing this vesicle with the cerebellum. The basilar (ventral) pons is dominated by the pontine nuclei, which are formed by a remarkable tangential migration: their neurons are born in the lower rhombic lip of the caudal hindbrain and stream rostrally and ventrally to settle in the basis pontis, a long migration guided by netrin, Slit, and other cues (worked out mainly in mouse, rodent data). The pontine tegmentum houses the locus coeruleus, raphe, parabrachial, and reticular nuclei, patterned by the same dorsoventral Shh-versus-roof-plate gradient as the rest of the brainstem.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The pons is a CAUSE-of-development-failure site in pontocerebellar hypoplasia, a group of inherited disorders (often from TSEN complex mutations) in which the pons and cerebellum fail to grow. It is a CASUALTY in central pontine myelinolysis (from overly rapid correction of hyponatraemia, producing locked-in syndrome), in basilar artery stroke, and in diffuse intrinsic pontine glioma of childhood. Its tegmental monoaminergic nuclei carry the selective degenerative vulnerability detailed below.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.1 LOCUS COERULEUS (LC)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The locus coeruleus arises very early from the dorsal rhombomere one region of the rostral hindbrain, its noradrenergic identity specified by the transcription factors Mash1 (Ascl1), Phox2a, and Phox2b acting in sequence; loss of Phox2b abolishes the nucleus (rodent data). It is one of the first neuronal groups to differentiate and to begin projecting, and its axons reach essentially the whole brain, making it a scaffold for the development of other regions. In humans it contains on the order of fifteen thousand neurons per side.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The locus coeruleus is a CASUALTY and one of the very first casualties in both Alzheimer and Parkinson disease. In Alzheimer disease it is arguably the earliest site of tau pathology, showing pretangle tau in Braak stage zero, before the entorhinal cortex, and it loses neurons progressively; in Parkinson disease it accumulates alpha-synuclein early and its loss contributes to the non-motor features. Its selective vulnerability is extreme and is attributed to its enormously long, thin, poorly myelinated, highly branched axons, its high metabolic and oxidative load, its neuromelanin content, and its position as a first responder to circulating and inflammatory insults; neuromelanin-sensitive magnetic resonance imaging of the LC is now a MARKER of early neurodegeneration.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.2 RAPHE NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The serotonergic neurons of the raphe are among the earliest-born monoaminergic neurons, arising in the ventral hindbrain on either side of the floor plate under Shh, with their serotonergic identity specified by Nkx2.2, Gata2/3, Lmx1b, and the terminal selector Pet1 (Fev); loss of Pet1 removes most serotonin neurons (rodent data). The rostral cluster becomes the dorsal and median raphe supplying the forebrain, and the caudal cluster supplies the spinal cord. Early serotonin acts as a developmental signal shaping the wiring of other regions, so raphe function affects circuit formation broadly.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The raphe is central to mood disorders as the target of the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, and altered serotonergic signalling is a CAUSE-level contributor to depression and anxiety, though the raphe is more often dysregulated than destroyed there, so its role is partly that of a MARKER. It degenerates in Parkinson and Alzheimer disease, contributing to depression and sleep disturbance, where it is a CASUALTY. Caudal (medullary) serotonergic neurons are implicated in the sudden infant death syndrome, where serotonergic abnormalities impair protective responses to asphyxia, a CAUSE-level developmental vulnerability.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.3 PONTINE NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The pontine nuclei are formed by one of the longest tangential neuronal migrations in the brain: their neurons are born in the caudal rhombic lip, express Barhl1 and are guided by the netrin-DCC and Slit-Robo systems as they stream anteriorly around the hindbrain to accumulate in the ventral pons (established in mouse, rodent data). They then receive the massive corticopontine projection, on the order of twenty million fibres, which vastly outnumbers the corticospinal tract, and relay it as mossy fibres to the contralateral cerebellum. Their sheer size is the developmental statement of how dependent the cortex becomes on the cerebellum.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The pontine nuclei fail to form or remain hypoplastic in pontocerebellar hypoplasia, an inherited developmental disorder (frequently TSEN54 and related mutations) in which this is a CAUSE-level failure of assembly. They are a CASUALTY in central pontine myelinolysis and in basilar stroke, which sever the cortico-ponto-cerebellar relay and produce ataxia and, in severe pontine damage, locked-in syndrome. They have no primary neurodegenerative disease of adulthood.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.4 PEDUNCULOPONTINE AND LATERODORSAL TEGMENTAL NUCLEI (PPN, LDT)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: These cholinergic nuclei develop in the pontomesencephalic tegmentum and are part of the ascending arousal system and the mesencephalic locomotor region. Their cholinergic identity is specified within isthmic and rostral hindbrain territory; detailed lineage and birthdating are established mainly in rodent (rodent data), and a precise human timeline is not firmly fixed. They generate the ponto-geniculo-occipital waves of rapid eye movement sleep and are active in waking and REM.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The PPN and LDT are a CASUALTY in Parkinson disease and the atypical Parkinsonian syndromes, where cholinergic neuron loss contributes to gait freezing, postural instability, and falls, and their degeneration correlates with the rapid-eye-movement sleep behaviour disorder that often precedes Parkinsonism. Cholinergic loss here contributes to the cognitive and arousal deficits of Parkinson disease dementia and progressive supranuclear palsy. The PPN is an experimental deep brain stimulation target for gait freezing.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.5 PARABRACHIAL NUCLEUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The parabrachial nucleus develops in the dorsolateral pons around the superior cerebellar peduncle. It is a molecularly diverse cluster of genetically distinct subnuclei serving interoception, taste, pain affect, thermoregulation, fluid balance, and the arousal response to rising carbon dioxide. Its subnuclear diversity and connections are being mapped by genetic tools mainly in mouse (rodent data), and a human developmental timeline is not separately established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The parabrachial nucleus mediates the aversive quality of nausea, breathlessness, and pain, and its carbon-dioxide-sensing neurons drive the arousal that normally protects against asphyxia during sleep; failure of this arousal is implicated in the sudden infant death syndrome and in obstructive sleep apnoea outcomes, a CAUSE-level protective circuit. It relays the opioid-induced suppression of the carbon-dioxide arousal response relevant to opioid respiratory death. It is a CASUALTY in lateral pontine stroke. Much of its clinical importance is defined functionally rather than by a named degeneration.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.6 TRIGEMINAL NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The trigeminal sensory nuclei form a long column extending from the midbrain (mesencephalic nucleus, unusually containing primary sensory neuron cell bodies inside the central nervous system) through the pons (principal sensory nucleus) into the medulla and upper spinal cord (spinal trigeminal nucleus). The primary afferents come from the trigeminal ganglion, which is of mixed neural crest and placode origin. The somatotopic face map is assembled prenatally; in rodents the whisker-related barrelette map forms in the first postnatal days (rodent data) and its human analogue matures prenatally.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The trigeminal system is the seat of trigeminal neuralgia, in which a vascular loop compressing and demyelinating the trigeminal root, or a demyelinating plaque, produces paroxysmal facial pain; here the demyelination is a CAUSE. It is a CASUALTY in multiple sclerosis (a plaque at the root entry zone is a classic cause of trigeminal neuralgia in the young) and in lateral medullary (Wallenberg) stroke, which knocks out the spinal trigeminal nucleus and abolishes facial pain and temperature sensation ipsilaterally. Herpes zoster reactivates in the trigeminal ganglion, giving ophthalmic shingles.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.6 THE SUPERIOR OLIVARY COMPLEX (SOC)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The superior olivary complex develops in the caudal pontine tegmentum and is the first place the signals from the two ears are compared. Its exquisite microsecond-precision circuitry for interaural time differences (the medial superior olive) and level differences (the lateral superior olive) is assembled with a precision that depends on early spontaneous activity from the cochlea before hearing onset and then on acoustic experience; the calibration of these binaural maps is a well-studied case of activity-dependent refinement, largely in rodent, gerbil, and chick (rodent, gerbil, and avian data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The superior olivary complex has no signature primary human degeneration, which should be stated rather than glossed. It is a CASUALTY in brainstem stroke and in central auditory processing disorders, and its olivocochlear efferent system is implicated in the modulation of cochlear gain and possibly in tinnitus and noise protection. Because its binaural computation is calibrated in early life, congenital or early conductive hearing loss disrupts its development, a developmental CASUALTY that underlies deficits in sound localisation.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.7 THE VESTIBULAR NUCLEI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The vestibular nuclei develop in the dorsal pons and medulla from the alar plate and receive the vestibular nerve from Scarpa's ganglion. The vestibular system is functional early, being among the first sensory systems to operate, and the vestibulo-ocular reflex is present at birth. The nuclei integrate vestibular, visual, and proprioceptive signals, and this multisensory calibration is refined postnatally as an infant gains head control and locomotion.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The vestibular nuclei are a CASUALTY in brainstem stroke (lateral medullary and pontine syndromes), in demyelination, and in Wernicke encephalopathy, producing vertigo, nystagmus, and imbalance. Downbeat and other central nystagmus localise to the vestibulocerebellar and vestibular nuclear circuitry. They participate in vestibular migraine and in the central compensation that follows peripheral vestibular loss. They have no primary selective degeneration of their own; most disease reaching them is vascular, demyelinating, or nutritional.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.8 THE NUCLEUS INCERTUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The nucleus incertus is a pontine, largely GABAergic and relaxin-three-expressing nucleus that paces hippocampal theta and signals arousal and stress. It is a comparatively recently characterised structure, and its developmental origin and birthdate are not established; no honest developmental timeline for it can be stated at present. What would settle it is genetic fate-mapping of its relaxin-three neurons, work not yet done in detail.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The nucleus incertus is implicated, mainly through rodent work (rodent data), in stress, anxiety, arousal, and possibly in the hippocampal theta disturbances relevant to memory and to alcohol seeking, where it may be a CAUSE-level modulator. There is no named human disease of the nucleus incertus, and this should be stated plainly rather than filled in. This is an intentionally short entry, matching how little is established.

ENTRY: 2.4.2.9 THE NUCLEUS PREPOSITUS HYPOGLOSSI

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The nucleus prepositus hypoglossi lies in the dorsal medulla near the midline and functions as the neural integrator for horizontal gaze, converting an eye-velocity command into a held eye position. Its integrator function depends on a recurrent network whose tuning is calibrated by visual and vestibular feedback across development; the mechanism of the integrator has been studied in goldfish, cat, and monkey (lower-vertebrate and primate data), and a specific human developmental timeline is not separately established.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: Damage or dysfunction of the horizontal neural integrator produces gaze-evoked nystagmus, in which the eyes drift back toward centre and cannot hold an eccentric position. It is a CASUALTY in brainstem stroke, in demyelination, in Wernicke encephalopathy, and in drug toxicity (alcohol, anticonvulsants) that impairs the integrator. It has no primary degeneration of its own; its clinical signature is the failure of gaze-holding.

ENTRY: 2.4.3 THE MEDULLA

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The medulla (myelencephalon) is the caudal hindbrain, patterned by the most posterior rhombomeres under the highest Hox-gene codes. Its dorsal alar plate gives the sensory nuclei (the solitary and dorsal column nuclei, the vestibular and cochlear nuclei) and the caudal rhombic lip gives the precerebellar nuclei including the inferior olive; its ventral basal plate gives the visceromotor and branchiomotor nuclei and the reticular respiratory and cardiovascular groups. Because the medulla contains the respiratory rhythm generator and cardiovascular centres, its assembly is life-critical, and several of its nuclei depend on the transcription factors Phox2b, Egr2 (Krox20), and Hox genes (worked out largely in mouse, rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The medulla is a CAUSE site in congenital central hypoventilation syndrome, where PHOX2B mutation disrupts the development of its respiratory and autonomic neurons. It is a CASUALTY in lateral and medial medullary stroke, in the medullary onset of Wallenberg syndrome, and in the early Braak-stage alpha-synuclein pathology of Parkinson disease, which may begin in the dorsal motor vagal nucleus. Its respiratory and cardiovascular centres make even small medullary lesions potentially fatal.

ENTRY: 2.4.3.1 THE VAGAL COMPLEX: THE NUCLEUS TRACTUS SOLITARIUS (NTS) AND THE DORSAL MOTOR NUCLEUS OF THE VAGUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The nucleus tractus solitarius forms from the dorsal (alar) medulla as the first central relay of all visceral afference, and the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus forms from the ventral (basal) visceromotor column, its parasympathetic identity requiring the transcription factor Phox2b (rodent data). The vagal afferents arrive from the nodose (inferior vagal) ganglion, which is placode-derived. Roughly eighty percent of vagal fibres are afferent, so the vagus is overwhelmingly a sensory nerve. The visceral topography of the NTS (cardiovascular, respiratory, gastrointestinal, and gustatory zones) is laid down prenatally.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus is, on Braak's staging, one of the earliest sites of alpha-synuclein (Lewy) pathology in Parkinson disease, consistent with the hypothesis that the pathology may ascend from the gut through the vagus, so it is both a CASUALTY and, if the gut-first hypothesis holds, an early station in the disease's spread. The vagal complex is a CASUALTY in multiple system atrophy and diabetic autonomic neuropathy, producing gastroparesis, blood-pressure instability, and impaired baroreflex. PHOX2B mutation disrupts its development in congenital central hypoventilation syndrome, a CAUSE.

ENTRY: 2.4.3.2 AREA POSTREMA

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The area postrema develops at the caudal floor of the fourth ventricle as a circumventricular organ, meaning it deliberately lacks a normal blood-brain barrier so it can sample the blood directly. This fenestrated vasculature is a specialised developmental feature, and the area postrema serves as the chemoreceptor trigger zone for vomiting. Its development as a barrier-free port is a designed hole, not a defect, and it expresses receptors for many circulating emetic signals.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The area postrema is the anatomical reason chemotherapy and many drugs cause nausea, and it is the target of antiemetic drugs; this is pharmacology exploiting its barrier-free access rather than a disease. It is a CASUALTY in neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder, where aquaporin-four antibodies attack this aquaporin-four-rich region and produce the area postrema syndrome of intractable hiccups, nausea, and vomiting, sometimes the presenting feature of the disease. Its lack of a barrier also makes it an early portal for circulating toxins and inflammatory signals.

ENTRY: 2.4.3.3 INFERIOR OLIVARY NUCLEUS

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The inferior olivary nucleus is a precerebellar nucleus born in the caudal rhombic lip that migrates to the ventral medulla and sends the climbing fibres to the contralateral cerebellum, one climbing fibre per Purkinje cell, forming the substrate of supervised learning. The one-to-one climbing-fibre-to-Purkinje-cell relationship is achieved developmentally by an initial multiple innervation that is pruned to a single fibre in early postnatal life, a classic example of activity-dependent synapse elimination (studied in rodent, rodent data).

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The inferior olive undergoes a unique and paradoxical response to injury: lesions of the Guillain-Mollaret triangle (the dentato-rubro-olivary loop) cause hypertrophic olivary degeneration, in which the olive enlarges rather than shrinks, producing palatal tremor. Here it is a CASUALTY that responds by hypertrophy. It is involved in essential tremor and in various ataxias where olivocerebellar signalling is disturbed. It is one of the few structures that gets bigger when part of its circuit is cut, which makes its imaging appearance distinctive.

ENTRY: 2.4.3.4 VENTRAL RESPIRATORY GROUP AND THE PRE-BOTZINGER COMPLEX

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The ventral respiratory column, including the pre-Botzinger complex that generates the inspiratory rhythm, develops in the ventrolateral medulla. Its core rhythmogenic neurons derive from progenitors expressing the homeobox gene Dbx1, and in mouse, deletion of Dbx1 prevents the pre-Botzinger complex from forming and the animals die at birth of asphyxia (rodent data). The rhythm is present and functional before birth, so the fetus performs breathing movements in utero. Transcription factors including Phox2b and the peptides that mark these neurons pattern the column.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: This region is the CAUSE site of congenital central hypoventilation syndrome (Ondine's curse), in which PHOX2B mutation impairs the development of the chemosensory and respiratory neurons so that automatic breathing fails, especially in sleep. It is implicated in the sudden infant death syndrome, where brainstem respiratory and serotonergic abnormalities impair the response to asphyxia. It is the site at which opioids suppress breathing, the mechanism of opioid respiratory death, and it degenerates in multiple system atrophy, contributing to nocturnal stridor and sudden death. Its destruction is immediately fatal.

ENTRY: 2.4.3.5 RETICULAR FORMATION

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The reticular formation is a phylogenetically ancient, diffuse core running the length of the brainstem, developing from the central (medial and lateral) columns of the basal and alar plates. It is not a single nucleus but a network of loosely bounded groups, and its ascending arousal projections form the anatomical basis of the ascending reticular activating system. Because it is diffuse and defined more by connectivity than by sharp borders, its development is described in general patterning terms rather than by a discrete birthdate.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS: The reticular formation is the CASUALTY whose damage produces coma: the demonstration by Moruzzi and Magoun that its stimulation wakes a sleeping animal and its destruction produces irreversible coma is foundational. It is damaged in brainstem stroke, in traumatic brain injury with brainstem involvement, in central herniation, and in the disorders of consciousness (coma, vegetative and minimally conscious states). Its cardiovascular, vomiting, and micturition centres and its control of postural tone make it broadly life-critical. It has no single primary degeneration; it is a casualty of insults that reach the brainstem core.

ENTRY: 2.4.3.6 THE ROSTRAL VENTROLATERAL MEDULLA (RVLM)

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY: The rostral ventrolateral medulla, containing the C1 adrenergic neurons and the presympathetic neurons that set resting blood pressure, develops in the ventral medulla and depends on the same brainstem autonomic transcriptional programme (including Phox2b) that specifies the other visceromotor groups (rodent data). It is the final integrating hub of the baroreflex, receiving inhibitory input from the solitary nucleus and driving the sympathetic preganglionic neurons of the spinal cord.
